Factoring Derivation Spaces via Intersection Types (Extended Version) Thanks: Work partially supported by CONICET.
Pablo Barenbaum, Gonzalo Ciruelos

Abstract
In typical non-idempotent intersection type systems, proof normalization is not confluent. In this paper we introduce a confluent non-idempotent intersection type system for the λ \lambda -calculus. Typing derivations are presented using proof term syntax. The system enjoys good properties: subject reduction, strong normalization, and a very regular theory of residuals. A correspondence with the λ \lambda -calculus is established by simulation theorems. The machinery of non-idempotent intersection types allows us to track the usage of resources required to obtain an answer. In particular, it induces a notion of garbage : a computation is garbage if it does not contribute to obtaining an answer. Using these notions, we show that the derivation space of a λ \lambda -term may be factorized using a variant of the Grothendieck construction for semilattices. This means, in particular, that any derivation in the λ \lambda -calculus can be uniquely written as a garbage-free prefix followed by garbage.



1
Introduction

Our goal in this paper is attempting to
understand the spaces of computations of programs.
Consider a hypothetical functional programming language
with arithmetic expressions and tuples.
All the possible computations starting from the tuple

(1+1,\ 2*3+1)

can be arranged to form its “space of computations”:

\textstyle{(1+1,\ 2*3+1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(1+1,\ 6+1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(1+1,\ 7)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(2,\ 2*3+1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(2,\ 6+1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(2,\ 7)}

In this case, the space of computations is quite easy to understand,
because the subexpressions
(1+1)
and
(2*3+1)
cannot interact with each other.
Indeed, the space of computations of a tuple
(A,B)
can always be understood as
the
product
of the spaces of
A
and
B
.
In the general case, however, the space of computations of a program may have a
much more complex structure.
For example, it is not easy to characterize the space of computations of a function application
f(A)
.
The difficulty is that
f
may use the value of
A
zero, one, or
possibly many times.

The quintessential functional programming language
is the pure
\lambda
-calculus.
Computations in the
\lambda
-calculus have been thoroughly
studied since its conception in the 1930s.
The well-known theorem by Church and Rosser
[
10
]

states that
\beta
-reduction in the
\lambda
-calculus is
confluent
,
which means, in particular, that terminating programs have unique normal forms.
Another result by Curry and Feys
[
12
]

states that computations in the

\lambda
-calculus may be
standardized
,
meaning that they may be converted into a computation in canonical form.
A refinement of this theorem by Lévy
[
25
]

asserts that the canonical computation thus obtained is equivalent to the
original one in a strong sense, namely that they are
permutation equivalent
.
In a series of papers
[
29
,
30
,
31
,
32
]
,
Melliès generalized many of these results to the abstract setting of
axiomatic rewrite systems
.

Let us discuss “spaces of computations” more precisely.
The
derivation space
of an object
x
in some rewriting system
is the set of all
derivations
,
i.e.
sequences of rewrite steps, starting from
x
.
In this paper, we will be interested in the pure
\lambda
-calculus,
and we will study
finite
derivations only.
In the
\lambda
-calculus,
a transitive relation between derivations may be defined, the
prefix order
.
A derivation
\rho
is a prefix of a derivation
\sigma
,
written
\rho\sqsubseteq\sigma
,
whenever
\rho
performs less computational work than
\sigma
.
Formally,
\rho\sqsubseteq\sigma
is defined to hold whenever the
projection

\rho/\sigma

is empty
1
1

1

The notion of projection defined by means of residuals
is the standard one, see
e.g.

[
4
, Chapter 12]

or
[
33
, Section 8.7]
.
.
For example, if
K=\lambda x.\lambda y.x
,
the derivation space of the term
(\lambda x.xx)(Kz)

can be depicted with the
reduction graph
below.
Derivations are directed paths in the reduction graph,
and
\rho
is a prefix of
\sigma
if there is a directed path from the
target of
\rho
to the target of
\sigma
.
For instance,
SR_{2}
is a prefix of
RS^{\prime}T^{\prime}
:

\textstyle{(\lambda x.xx)(Kz)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{S}
\textstyle{(\lambda x.xx)(\lambda y.z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{\prime}}
\textstyle{(Kz)(\lambda y.z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R^{\prime}_{2}}
\textstyle{(\lambda y.z)(\lambda y.z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T^{\prime}}
\textstyle{(Kz)(Kz)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{1}}
\scriptstyle{R_{2}}
\textstyle{(\lambda y.z)(Kz)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{z}

Remark that
\sqsubseteq
is reflexive and transitive but not antisymmetric,

i.e.
it is a quasi-order but not an order.
For example
RS^{\prime}\sqsubseteq SR_{1}R_{2}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq RS^{\prime}

but
RS^{\prime}\neq SR_{1}R_{2}^{\prime}
.
Antisymmetry may be recovered as usual when in presence of a quasi-order,
by working modulo
permutation equivalence
:
two derivations
\rho
and
\sigma

are said to be permutation equivalent,
written
\rho\equiv\sigma
, if
\rho\sqsubseteq\sigma

and
\sigma\sqsubseteq\rho
.
Working modulo permutation equivalence is reasonable because Lévy’s
formulation of the
standardization theorem ensures that permutation equivalence is decidable.

Derivation spaces are known to exhibit various
regularities
[
25
,
35
,
24
,
28
,
26
,
2
]
.
In his PhD thesis, Lévy
[
25
]
showed that the derivation space of a term is
an upper semilattice: any two derivations
\rho,\sigma
from a term
t

have a
least upper bound

\rho\sqcup\sigma
, defined as
\rho(\sigma/\rho)
,
unique up to permutation equivalence.
On the other hand, the derivation space of a term
t
is not an easy structure to understand in general
2
2

2

Problem 2 in
the RTA List of Open Problems
[
13
]
poses
the open-ended question of investigating the properties of “spectra”,
i.e.
derivation spaces.
.
For example, relating the derivation space of an application
ts

with the derivation spaces of
t
and
s
appears to be a hard problem.
Lévy also noted that the
greatest lower bound
of two derivations does
not necessarily exist, meaning that the derivation space of a term does not form a lattice in general.
Even when it forms a lattice, it may not necessarily be a
distributive
lattice, as observed for example by
Laneve
[
24
]
.
In
[
29
]
, Melliès showed that
derivation spaces in any rewriting system satisfying certain axioms
may be factorized using two spaces,
one of
external
and one of
internal
derivations.

The difficulty to understand derivation spaces is due to three pervasive phenomena
of
interaction
between computations.
The first phenomenon is
duplication
:
in the reduction graph of above, the step
S
duplicates the step
R
,
resulting in two copies of
R
: the steps
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
.
In such situation, one says that
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
are
residuals
of
R
,
and, conversely,
R
is an
ancestor
of
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
.
The second phenomenon is
erasure
:
in the diagram above, the step
T
erases the step
R^{\prime}_{1}
,
resulting in no copies of
R^{\prime}_{1}
.
The third phenomenon is
creation
:
in the diagram above, the step
R_{2}
creates the step
T
,
meaning that
T
is not a residual of a step that existed prior
to executing
R_{2}
; that is,
T
has no ancestor.

These three interaction phenomena, especially duplication and erasure,
are intimately related with the management of
resources
.
In this work, we aim to explore the hypothesis that
having an explicit
representation of resource management may provide insight on
the structure of derivation spaces
.

There are many existing
\lambda
-calculi that deal with resource management explicitly
[
6
,
15
,
20
,
21
]
,
most of which draw inspiration from Girard’s Linear Logic
[
18
]
.
Recently, calculi endowed with

non-idempotent intersection type systems
,
have received some attention
[
14
,
5
,
7
,
8
,
19
,
34
,
22
]
.
These type systems are able to statically capture non-trivial
dynamic properties of terms, particularly
normalization
,
while at the same time being amenable to elementary proof techniques by induction.
Intersection types were originally proposed by
Coppo and Dezani-Ciancaglini
[
11
]

to study termination in the
\lambda
-calculus.
They are characterized by the presence of an
intersection
type constructor
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}
.

Non-idempotent
intersection type systems are distinguished from their usual idempotent
counterparts by the fact that intersection
is not declared to be idempotent,
i.e.

\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{A}
are not equivalent types.
Rather, intersection behaves like a multiplicative connective in linear logic.
Arguments to functions are typed many times, typically once
per each time that the argument will be used.
Non-idempotent intersection types were originally formulated by
Gardner
[
17
]
,
and later reintroduced by de Carvalho
[
9
]
.

In this paper, we will use a non-idempotent intersection type system
based on system
\mathcal{W}
of
[
8
]

(called system
\mathcal{H}
in
[
7
]
).
Let us recall its definition.
Terms are as usual in the
\lambda
-calculus (
t::=x\mid\lambda x.t\mid t\,t
).
Types
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C},\ldots
are defined by the grammar:

\begin{array}[]{rcl@{\hspace{2cm}}rcl}\mathcal{A}&::=&\alpha\ \mid\ \mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{A}\hfil\hskip 56.9055pt&\mathcal{M}&::=&[\mathcal{A}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{with $n\geq 0$}\\
\end{array}

where
\alpha
ranges over one of denumerably many
base types
,
and
\mathcal{M}
represents a
multiset of types
.
Here
[\mathcal{A}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}
denotes the multiset
\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{n}

with their respective multiplicities.
A multiset
[\mathcal{A}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}
intuitively stands for the (non-idempotent) intersection
\mathcal{A}_{1}\cap\ldots\cap\mathcal{A}_{n}
.
The
sum of multisets

\mathcal{M}+\mathcal{N}
is defined as their union (adding multiplicities).
A
typing context

\Gamma
is a partial function mapping variables to multisets of types.
The domain of
\Gamma
is the set of variables
x
such that
\Gamma(x)
is defined.
We assume that typing contexts always have
finite domain
and hence they may be written as

x_{1}:\mathcal{M}_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:\mathcal{M}_{n}
.
The
sum of contexts

\Gamma+\Delta
is their pointwise sum,

i.e.

(\Gamma+\Delta)(x):=\Gamma(x)+\Delta(x)
if
\Gamma(x)
and
\Delta(x)
are both defined,

(\Gamma+\Delta)(x):=\Gamma(x)
if
\Delta(x)
is undefined,
and
(\Gamma+\Delta)(x):=\Delta(x)
if
\Gamma(x)
is undefined.
We write
\Gamma+_{i=1}^{n}\Delta_{i}
to abbreviate
\Gamma+\Delta_{1}+\ldots+\Delta_{n}
.
The
disjoint sum of contexts

\Gamma\oplus\Delta
stands for
\Gamma+\Delta
, provided that
the domains of
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are disjoint.
A
typing judgment
is a triple
\Gamma\vdash t:\mathcal{A}
,
representing the knowledge that the term
t
has type
\mathcal{A}
in the context
\Gamma
.
Type assignment rules for system
\mathcal{W}
are as follows.

Definition 1
(System
\mathcal{W}
)

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 20.09222pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=40.18445pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\texttt{{var}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:[\mathcal{A}]\vdash\mathcal{A}$}}}}\end{array}\hskip 4.26773pt\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\oplus(x:\mathcal{M})\vdash t:\mathcal{A}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=69.34674pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\texttt{{lam}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 4.75345pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.t:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{A}$}}}}\end{array}\hskip 4.26773pt\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:[\mathcal{B}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}\to\mathcal{A}\hskip 14.22636pt(\Delta_{i}\vdash s:\mathcal{B}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=140.24174pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\texttt{{app}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 35.85028pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma+_{i=1}^{n}\Delta_{i}\vdash t\,s:\mathcal{A}$}}}}\end{array}

Observe that the
app
rule has
n+1
premises, where
n\geq 0
.
System
\mathcal{W}
enjoys various properties, nicely summarized in
[
8
]
.

There are two obstacles to adopting system
\mathcal{W}
for studying derivation spaces.
The first obstacle is mostly a matter of presentation—typing derivations use a tree-like notation,
which is cumbersome. One would like to have an alternative notation based on proof terms.
For example, one may define proof terms for the typing rules above using the
syntax
\pi::=x^{\mathcal{A}}\mid\lambda x.\pi\mid\pi[\pi,\ldots,\pi]
,
in such a way that
x^{\mathcal{A}}
encodes an application of the
var
axiom,

\lambda x.\pi
encodes an application of the
lam
rule to the typing derivation encoded by
\pi
,
and
\pi_{1}[\pi_{2},\ldots,\pi_{n}]
encodes an application of the

app
rule to the typing derivations encoded by
\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\ldots,\pi_{n}
.
For example, using this notation

\lambda x.x^{[\alpha,\alpha]\to\beta}[x^{\alpha},x^{\alpha}]

would represent the following typing derivation:

{\small\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip 4.62497pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip 4.62497pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 54.42989pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=108.85977pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\texttt{{var}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:[\alpha,\alpha]\to\beta\vdash x:[\alpha,\alpha]\to\beta$}}}}\end{array}\hskip-8.5359pt\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip 4.62497pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 26.84464pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=53.68927pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\texttt{{var}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:[\alpha]\vdash x:\alpha$}}}}\end{array}\hskip-8.5359pt\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip 4.62497pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 26.84464pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=53.68927pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\texttt{{var}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:[\alpha]\vdash x:\alpha$}}}}\end{array}\vskip 2.84544pt$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=280.87448pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\texttt{{app}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 86.2122pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:[[\alpha,\alpha]\to\beta,\alpha,\alpha]\vdash xx:\beta$}}}}\end{array}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=310.40213pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\texttt{{lam}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 95.19482pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\lambda x.xx:[[\alpha,\alpha]\to\beta,\alpha,\alpha]\to\beta$}}}}\end{array}}

The second obstacle is a major one for our purposes:
proof normalization
in this system is not confluent.
The reason is that applications take multiple arguments, and a
\beta
-reduction step must choose a way to
distribute these arguments among the occurrences of the formal parameters.
For instance, the following critical pair cannot be closed:

(\lambda x.y^{[\alpha]\to[\alpha]\to\beta}[x^{\alpha}][x^{\alpha}])[z^{[\gamma]\to\alpha}[z^{\gamma}],z^{[]\to\alpha}[]]

\textstyle{y^{[\alpha]\to[\alpha]\to\beta}[z^{[\gamma]\to\alpha}[z^{\gamma}]][z^{[]\to\alpha}[]]}
\textstyle{y^{[\alpha]\to[\alpha]\to\beta}[z^{[]\to\alpha}[]][z^{[\gamma]\to\alpha}[z^{\gamma}]]}

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows:

•

In Sec.
2
, we review some standard notions of order and rewriting theory.

•

In Sec.
3
, we introduce a confluent calculus
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
based on system
\mathcal{W}
.
The desirable properties of system
\mathcal{W}
of
[
8
]
still hold in
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
.
Moreover,
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
is confluent.
We impose confluence forcibly, by decorating subtrees with distinct labels, so that
a
\beta
-reduction step may distribute the arguments in a unique way.
Derivation spaces in
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
have very regular structure,
namely they are distributive lattices.

•

In Sec.
4
, we establish a correspondence between derivation spaces in the

\lambda
-calculus and the
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
-calculus via simulation theorems,
which defines a morphism of upper semilattices.

•

In Sec.
5
, we introduce the notion of a garbage derivation.
Roughly, a derivation in the
\lambda
-calculus is
garbage
if it maps to
an empty derivation in the
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
-calculus.
This gives rise to an orthogonal notion of
garbage-free
derivation.
The notion of garbage-free derivation is closely related
with the notions of
needed step

[
33
, Section 8.6]
,

typed occurrence of a redex

[
8
]
,
and
external
derivation
[
29
]
.
Using this notion of garbage we prove a
factorization theorem

reminiscent of Melliès’
[
29
]
.
The upper semilattice of derivations of a term in the
\lambda
-calculus
is factorized using a variant of the Grothendieck construction.
Every derivation is uniquely decomposed as a garbage-free prefix followed by a garbage suffix.

•

In Sec.
6
, we conclude.

Note.
Proofs including a
\clubsuit
symbol are spelled out in detail in the appendix.

2
Preliminaries

We recall some standard definitions.
An
upper semilattice
is a poset
(A,\leq)

with a least element or
bottom

\bot\in A
,
and such that for every two elements
a,b\in A

there is a least upper bound or
join

(a\lor b)\in A
.
A
lattice
is an upper semilattice
with a greatest element or
top

\top\in A
,
and such that for every two elements
a,b\in A

there is a greatest lower bound or
meet

(a\land b)\in A
.
A lattice is
distributive
if
\land
distributes over
\lor
and vice versa.
A
morphism
of upper semilattices
is given by a monotonic function
f:A\to B
,

i.e.

a\leq b
implies
f(a)\leq f(b)
,
preserving the bottom element,
i.e.

f(\bot)=\bot
,
and joins,
i.e.

f(a\lor b)=f(a)\lor f(b)
for all
a,b\in A
.
Similarly for morphisms of lattices.
Any poset
(A,\leq)
forms a category whose objects are the elements of
A

and morphisms are of the form
a\hookrightarrow b
for all
a\leq b
.
The category of posets with monotonic functions is denoted by
\mathsf{Poset}
.
In fact, we regard it as a 2-category: given morphisms
f,g:A\to B
of posets,
we have that
f\leq g
whenever
f(a)\leq g(a)
for all
a\in A
.

An
axiomatic rewrite system
(
cf.

[
28
, Def. 2.1]
)
is given by a set of objects
\mathsf{Obj}
, a set of steps
\mathsf{Stp}
,
two functions
\mathsf{src},\mathsf{tgt}:\mathsf{Stp}\to\mathsf{Obj}
indicating the source and target of each step,
and a
residual function

(/)
such that given any two steps
R,S\in\mathsf{Stp}

with the same source, yields a set of steps
R/S
such that
\mathsf{src}(R^{\prime})=\mathsf{tgt}(S)

for all
R^{\prime}\in R/S
.
Steps are ranged over by
R,S,T,\ldots
.
A step
R^{\prime}\in R/S
is called a
residual
of
R
after
S
,
and
R
is called an
ancestor
of
R^{\prime}
.
Steps are
coinitial
(resp.
cofinal
) if they have the same source (resp. target).
A
derivation
is a possibly empty sequence of composable steps
R_{1}\ldots R_{n}
.
Derivations are ranged over by
\rho,\sigma,\tau,\ldots
.
The functions
\mathsf{src}
and
\mathsf{tgt}
are extended to derivations.
Composition of derivations is defined when
\mathsf{tgt}(\rho)=\mathsf{src}(\sigma)
and written
\rho\,\sigma
.
Residuals after a derivation can be defined by

R_{n}\in R_{0}/S_{1}\ldots S_{n}

if and only if there exist
R_{1},\ldots,R_{n-1}

such that
R_{i+1}\in R_{i}/S_{i+1}
for all
0\leq i\leq n-1
.
Let
\mathcal{M}
be a set of coinitial steps.
A
development
of
\mathcal{M}
is a (possibly infinite) derivation
R_{1}\ldots R_{n}\ldots

such that for every index
i
there exists a step
S\in\mathcal{M}

such that
R_{i}\in S/R_{1}\ldots R_{i-1}
.
A development is
complete
if it is maximal.

An
orthogonal
axiomatic rewrite system (
cf.

[
28
, Sec. 2.3]
)
has four additional axioms
3
3

3

In
[
28
]
,
Autoerasure is called Axiom A, Finite Residuals is called Axiom B, and Semantic Orthogonality is called PERM.
We follow the nomenclature of
[
1
]
.
:

1.

Autoerasure
.

R/R=\varnothing
for all
R\in\mathsf{Stp}
.

2.

Finite Residuals
.
The set
R/S
is finite for all coinitial
R,S\in\mathsf{Stp}
.

3.

Finite Developments
.
If
\mathcal{M}
is a set of coinitial steps,
all developments of
\mathcal{M}
are finite.

4.

Semantic Orthogonality
.
Let
R,S\in\mathsf{Stp}
be coinitial steps.
Then there exist a complete development
\rho
of
R/S

and a complete development
\sigma
of
S/R

such that
\rho
and
\sigma
are cofinal.
Moreover,
for every step
T\in\mathsf{Stp}
such that
T
is coinitial to
R
,
the following equality between sets holds:

T/(R\sigma)=T/(S\rho)
.

In
[
28
]
, Melliès develops
the theory of orthogonal axiomatic rewrite systems.
A notion of
projection

\rho/\sigma
may be defined between coinitial derivations,
essentially by setting
\epsilon/\sigma\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\epsilon

and
R\rho^{\prime}/\sigma\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(R/\sigma)(\rho^{\prime}/(\sigma/R))

where, by abuse of notation,
R/\sigma
stands for a (canonical) complete development
of the set
R/\sigma
.
Using this notion, one may define
a transitive relation of
prefix
(
\rho\sqsubseteq\sigma
),
a
permutation equivalence
relation (
\rho\equiv\sigma
),
and the
join
of derivations (
\rho\sqcup\sigma
).
Some of their properties are summed up in the figure
below:

Summary of properties of orthogonal axiomatic rewrite systems

{\small\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|}\hline\cr\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{0.1cm}}c@{\hspace{0.1cm}}l}\epsilon\,\rho\hskip 2.84544pt&=\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&\rho\\
\rho\,\epsilon\hskip 2.84544pt&=\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&\rho\\
\epsilon/\rho\hskip 2.84544pt&=\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&\epsilon\\
\rho/\epsilon\hskip 2.84544pt&=\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&\rho\\
\rho/\sigma\tau\hskip 2.84544pt&=\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&(\rho/\sigma)/\tau\\
\rho\sigma/\tau\hskip 2.84544pt&=\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&(\rho/\tau)(\sigma/(\tau/\rho))\\
\rho/\rho\hskip 2.84544pt&=\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&\epsilon\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{0.1cm}}c@{\hspace{0.1cm}}l}\rho\sqsubseteq\sigma\hskip 2.84544pt&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{\iff}\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&\rho/\sigma=\epsilon\\
\rho\equiv\sigma\hskip 2.84544pt&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{\iff}\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&\rho\sqsubseteq\sigma\land\sigma\sqsubseteq\rho\\
\rho\sqcup\sigma\hskip 2.84544pt&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&\rho(\sigma/\rho)\\
\rho\equiv\sigma\hskip 2.84544pt&\implies\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&\tau/\rho=\tau/\sigma\\
\rho\sqsubseteq\sigma\hskip 2.84544pt&\iff\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&\exists\tau.\ \rho\tau\equiv\sigma\\
\rho\sqsubseteq\sigma\hskip 2.84544pt&\iff\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&\rho\sqcup\sigma\equiv\sigma\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{0.1cm}}c@{\hspace{0.1cm}}l}\rho\sqsubseteq\sigma\hskip 2.84544pt&\implies\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&\rho/\tau\sqsubseteq\sigma/\tau\\
\rho\sqsubseteq\sigma\hskip 2.84544pt&\iff\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&\tau\rho\sqsubseteq\tau\sigma\\
\rho\sqcup\sigma\hskip 2.84544pt&\equiv\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&\sigma\sqcup\rho\\
(\rho\sqcup\sigma)\sqcup\tau\hskip 2.84544pt&=\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&\rho\sqcup(\sigma\sqcup\tau)\\
\rho\hskip 2.84544pt&\sqsubseteq\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&\rho\sqcup\sigma\\
(\rho\sqcup\sigma)/\tau\hskip 2.84544pt&=\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&(\rho/\tau)\sqcup(\sigma/\tau)\\
\end{array}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}}

Let
{[\rho]}=\{\sigma\ |\ \rho\equiv\sigma\}

denote the permutation equivalence class of
\rho
.
In an orthogonal axiomatic rewrite system,
the set
\mathbb{D}(x)=\{{[\rho]}\ |\ \mathsf{src}(\rho)=x\}

forms an upper semilattice
[
28
, Thm. 2.2,Thm. 2.3]
.
The order
{[\rho]}\sqsubseteq{[\sigma]}
is declared to hold if

\rho\sqsubseteq\sigma
,
the join is
{[\rho]}\sqcup{[\sigma]}={[\rho\sqcup\sigma]}
,
and the bottom is
\bot={[\epsilon]}
.
The
\lambda
-calculus is an example of an orthogonal axiomatic rewrite system.
Our structures of interest are the semilattices of derivations of the
\lambda
-calculus,
written
\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}(t)
for any given
\lambda
-term
t
.
As usual,
\beta
-reduction in the
\lambda
-calculus
is written
t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}s

and defined by the contextual closure of the axiom
(\lambda x.t)s\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}t\{x:=s\}
.

3
The Distributive
\lambda
-Calculus

In this section we introduce the

distributive
\lambda
-calculus
(
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
),
and we prove some basic results.
Terms of the
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
-calculus are typing derivations of a non-idempotent intersection type
system, written using proof term syntax.
The underlying type system is a variant of system
\mathcal{W}
of
[
7
,
8
]
,
the main difference being that
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}

uses
labels
and a suitable invariant on terms,
to ensure that the formal parameters of all functions
are in 1–1 correspondence with the actual arguments that they receive.

Definition 2
(Syntax of the
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
-calculus)

Let
\mathscr{L}=\{\ell,\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime},\ldots\}
be a denumerable set of labels.
The set of
types
is ranged over by
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C},\ldots
,
and defined inductively as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl@{\hspace{2cm}}rcl}\mathcal{A}&::=&\alpha^{\ell}\mid\mathcal{M}\overset{\ell}{\to}\mathcal{A}\hfil\hskip 56.9055pt&\mathcal{M}&::=&[\mathcal{A}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{with $n\geq 0$}\\
\end{array}

where
\alpha
ranges over one of denumerably many
base types
,
and
\mathcal{M}
represents a
multiset of types
.
In a type like
\alpha^{\ell}
and
\mathcal{M}\overset{\ell}{\to}\mathcal{A}
,
the label
\ell
is called the
external label
.
The
typing contexts
are defined as in Sec.
1
for system
\mathcal{W}
.
We write
\dom\Gamma
for the domain of
\Gamma
.
A type
\mathcal{A}
is said to
occur
inside another type
\mathcal{B}
,
written
\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{B}
, if
\mathcal{A}
is a subformula of
\mathcal{B}
.
This is extended to say that a type
\mathcal{A}
occurs in a multiset
[\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n}]
,
declaring that
\mathcal{A}\preceq[\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n}]
if
\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{B}_{i}
for some
i=1..n
,
and that a type
\mathcal{A}
occurs in a typing context
\Gamma
,
declaring that
\mathcal{A}\preceq\Gamma
if
\mathcal{A}\preceq\Gamma(x)
for some
x\in\dom\Gamma
.

The set of

terms
,
ranged over by
t,s,u,\ldots
, is given by the grammar

t::=x^{\mathcal{A}}\mid\lambda^{\ell}x.t\mid t\,\bar{t}
,
where
\bar{t}
represents a (possibly empty)
finite list
of terms.
The notations
[x_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}
,
[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
, and
\bar{x}

all stand simultaneously for multisets and for lists of elements.
Note that there is no confusion since we only work with
multisets of types
,
and with
lists of terms
.
The concatenation of the lists
\bar{x},\bar{y}
is denoted by
\bar{x}+\bar{y}
.
A sequence of
n
lists
(\bar{x}_{1},\ldots,\bar{x}_{n})
is a
partition
of
\bar{x}

if
\bar{x}_{1}+\ldots+\bar{x}_{n}
is a permutation of
\bar{x}
.
The set of
free variables
of a term
t

is written
\mathsf{fv}({t})
and defined as expected.
We also write
\mathsf{fv}({[t_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}})
for
\cup_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{fv}({t_{i}})
.
A
context
is a term
\mathtt{C}
with an occurrence of a distinguished
hole

\Box
.
We write
\mathtt{C}\langle t\rangle
for the capturing substitution of
\Box
by
t
.

Typing judgments
are triples
\Gamma\vdash t:\mathcal{A}
representing the knowledge
that the term
t
has type
\mathcal{A}
in the context
\Gamma
.
Type assignment rules are:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 30.58324pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=61.16647pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\texttt{{var}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:[\mathcal{A}]\vdash x^{\mathcal{A}}:\mathcal{A}$}}}}\end{array}\hskip 14.22636pt\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\oplus(x:\mathcal{M})\vdash t:\mathcal{B}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=74.60391pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\texttt{{lam}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.15271pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\lambda^{\ell}x.t:\mathcal{M}\overset{\ell}{\to}\mathcal{B}$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:[\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n}]\overset{\ell}{\to}\mathcal{A}\hskip 14.22636pt\left(\Delta_{i}\vdash s_{i}:\mathcal{B}_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{n}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=168.41037pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\texttt{{app}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 30.95287pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma+_{i=1}^{n}\Delta_{i}\vdash t[s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}]:\mathcal{A}$}}}}\end{array}

For example

\vdash\lambda^{1}x.x^{[\alpha^{2},\alpha^{3}]\overset{4}{\to}\beta^{5}}[x^{\alpha^{3}},x^{\alpha^{2}}]:[[\alpha^{2},\alpha^{3}]\overset{4}{\to}\beta^{5},\alpha^{2},\alpha^{3}]\overset{1}{\to}\beta^{5}

is a derivable judgment (using integer labels).

Remark 1
(Unique typing)

Let
\Gamma\vdash t:\mathcal{A}
and
\Delta\vdash t:\mathcal{B}
be derivable judgments.
Then
\Gamma=\Delta
and
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{B}
.
Moreover, the derivation trees coincide.

This can be checked by induction on
t
.
It means that
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
is an
à la Church
type system,
that is, types are an
intrinsic
property of the syntax of terms,
as opposed to an
à la Curry
type system like
{\mathcal{W}}
, in which
types are
extrinsic
properties that a given term might or might not have.

To define a confluent rewriting rule,
we impose a further constraint on the syntax of terms, called
correctness
.
The
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
-calculus will be defined over the set of correct terms.

Definition 3
(Correct terms)

A multiset of types
[\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{n}]
is
sequential

if the external labels of
\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\mathcal{A}_{j}
are different for all
i\neq j
.
A typing context
\Gamma
is sequential if
\Gamma(x)
is sequential for every
x\in\dom\Gamma
.
A term
t
is correct if it is typable and it verifies the following three conditions:

1.

Uniquely labeled lambdas.

If
\lambda^{\ell}x.s
and
\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.u

are subterms of
t
at different positions, then
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}

must be different labels.

2.

Sequential contexts.

If
s
is a subterm of
t
and
\Gamma\vdash s:\mathcal{A}

is derivable, then
\Gamma
must be sequential.

3.

Sequential types.

If
s
is a subterm of
t
,
the judgment
\Gamma\vdash s:\mathcal{A}
is derivable,
and there exists a type such that

(\mathcal{M}\overset{\ell}{\to}\mathcal{B}\preceq\Gamma)\lor(\mathcal{M}\overset{\ell}{\to}\mathcal{B}\preceq\mathcal{A})
,
then
\mathcal{M}
must be sequential.

The set of
correct
terms is denoted by
\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
.

For example,

x^{[\alpha^{1}]\overset{2}{\to}\beta^{3}}[x^{\alpha^{1}}]
is a correct term,

\lambda^{1}x.\lambda^{1}y.y^{\alpha^{2}}

is not a correct term since labels for lambdas are not unique,
and

\lambda^{1}x.x^{\alpha^{2}\overset{3}{\to}[\beta^{4},\beta^{4}]\overset{5}{\to}\gamma^{6}}
is not a correct term
since
[\beta^{4},\beta^{4}]
is not sequential.

Substitution is defined explicitly below.
If
t
is typable,

\mathtt{T}_{x}(t)

stands for the multiset
of types of the free occurrences of
x
in
t
.
If
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
are typable,

\mathtt{T}([t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}])
stands for the multiset
of types of
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
.
For example,

\mathtt{T}_{x}(x^{[\alpha^{1}]\overset{2}{\to}\beta^{3}}[x^{\alpha^{1}}])=\mathtt{T}([y^{\alpha^{1}},z^{[\alpha^{1}]\overset{2}{\to}\beta^{3}}])=[[\alpha^{1}]\overset{2}{\to}\beta^{3},\alpha^{1}]
.
To perform a substitution
t\{x:=[s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}]\}

we will require that
\mathtt{T}_{x}(t)=\mathtt{T}([s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}])
.

Definition 4
(Substitution)

Let
t
and
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
be correct terms such that
\mathtt{T}_{x}(t)=\mathtt{T}([s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}])
.
The capture-avoiding substitution of
x
in
t
by
\bar{s}=[s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}]

is denoted by
t\{x:=\bar{s}\}
and defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}x^{\mathcal{A}}\{x:=[s]\}&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&s\\
y^{\mathcal{A}}\{x:=[]\}&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&y^{\mathcal{A}}&\text{ if $x\neq y$}\\
(\lambda^{\ell}y.u)\{x:=\bar{s}\}&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\lambda^{\ell}y.u\{x:=\bar{s}\}&\text{ if $x\neq y$ and $y\not\in\mathsf{fv}({\bar{s}})$}\\
u_{0}[u_{j}]_{j=1}^{m}\{x:=\bar{s}\}&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&u_{0}\{x:=\bar{s}_{0}\}[u_{j}\{x:=\bar{s}_{j}\}]_{j=1}^{m}\end{array}

In the last case,
(\bar{s}_{0},\ldots,\bar{s}_{m})

is a partition of
\bar{s}

such that
\mathtt{T}_{x}(u_{j})=\mathtt{T}(\bar{s}_{j})
for all
j=0..m
.

Remark 2

Substitution is
type-directed
: the arguments
[s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}]

are propagated throughout the term
so that
s_{i}
reaches the free occurrence of
x

that has the same type as
s_{i}
.
Note that the definition of substitution requires that
\mathtt{T}_{x}(t)=\mathtt{T}([s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}])
,
which means that the types of the terms
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}

are in 1–1 correspondence with the types of the free occurrences of
x
.
Moreover, since
t
is a correct term,
the multiset
\mathtt{T}_{x}(t)
is sequential,
which implies in particular that each free occurrence of
x
has a different type.
Hence there is a
unique
correspondence matching the free occurrences of
x

with the arguments
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
that respects their types.
As a consequence, in the definition of substitution for an application

u_{0}[u_{j}]_{j=1}^{m}\{x:=\bar{s}\}

there is essentially a unique way to split
\bar{s}

into
n+1
lists
(\bar{s}_{0},\bar{s}_{1},\ldots,\bar{s}_{n})

in such a way that
\mathtt{T}_{x}(u_{i})=\mathtt{T}(\bar{s}_{i})
.
More precisely, if
(\bar{s}_{0},\bar{s}_{1},\ldots,\bar{s}_{n})

and
(\bar{u}_{0},\bar{u}_{1},\ldots,\bar{u}_{n})

are two partitions of
\bar{s}
with the stated property,
then
\bar{s}_{i}
is a permutation of
\bar{u}_{i}
for all
i=0..n
.
Using this argument, it is easy to check by induction on
t

that the value of
t\{x:=\bar{s}\}
is uniquely determined and
does not depend on this choice.

For example,

(x^{[\alpha^{1}]\overset{2}{\to}\beta^{3}}[x^{\alpha^{1}}])\{x:=[y^{[\alpha^{1}]\overset{2}{\to}\beta^{3}},z^{\alpha^{1}}]\}=y^{[\alpha^{1}]\overset{2}{\to}\beta^{3}}z^{\alpha^{1}}

while, on the other hand,

(x^{[\alpha^{1}]\overset{2}{\to}\beta^{3}}[x^{\alpha^{1}}])\{x:=[y^{\alpha^{1}},z^{[\alpha^{1}]\overset{2}{\to}\beta^{3}}]\}=z^{[\alpha^{1}]\overset{2}{\to}\beta^{3}}y^{\alpha^{1}}
.

The operation of substitution preserves term correctness and typability:

Lemma 1
(Subject Reduction)

If
\mathtt{C}\langle(\lambda^{\ell}x.t)\bar{s}\rangle
is a correct term
such that
the judgment
\Gamma\vdash\mathtt{C}\langle(\lambda^{\ell}x.t)\bar{s}\rangle:\mathcal{A}

is derivable, then
\mathtt{C}\langle t\{x:=\bar{s}\}\rangle
is correct
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathtt{C}\langle t\{x:=\bar{s}\}\rangle:\mathcal{A}

is derivable.

Proof

\clubsuit

By induction on
\mathtt{C}
.

Definition 5
(The
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
-calculus)

The
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
-calculus
is the rewriting system whose objects
are the set of correct terms
\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
.
The rewrite relation
\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}
is the closure under arbitrary contexts
of the rule
(\lambda^{\ell}x.t)\bar{s}\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}t\{x:=\bar{s}\}
.
Lem.
1
justifies that
\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}
is well-defined,
i.e.
that
the right-hand side is a correct term.
The label of a step is the label
\ell
decorating the contracted lambda.
We write
t\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}_{\texttt{{\#}}}}s
whenever
t\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}s
and the label of
the step is
\ell
.

Example 1

Let
I^{3}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\lambda^{3}x.x^{\alpha^{2}}

and
I^{4}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\lambda^{4}x.x^{\alpha^{2}}
.
The reduction graph of
the term

(\lambda^{1}x.x^{[\alpha^{2}]\overset{3}{\to}\alpha^{2}}[x^{\alpha^{2}}])[I^{3},I^{4}[z^{\alpha^{2}}]]
is:

\textstyle{(\lambda^{1}x.x^{[\alpha^{2}]\overset{3}{\to}\alpha^{2}}[x^{\alpha^{2}}])[I^{3},I^{4}[z^{\alpha^{2}}]]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{S}
\scriptstyle{4}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{I^{3}[I^{4}[z^{\alpha^{2}}]]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{3}
\scriptstyle{T}
\scriptstyle{4}
\scriptstyle{R^{\prime}}
\textstyle{I^{4}[z^{\alpha^{2}}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{4}
\scriptstyle{R^{\prime\prime}}
\textstyle{(\lambda^{1}x.x^{[\alpha^{2}]\overset{3}{\to}\alpha^{2}}[x^{\alpha^{2}}])[I^{3},z^{\alpha^{2}}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{S^{\prime}}
\textstyle{I^{3}[z^{\alpha^{2}}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{3}
\scriptstyle{T^{\prime}}
\textstyle{z^{\alpha^{2}}}

Note that numbers over arrows are the labels of the steps,
while
R,R^{\prime},S,...
are metalanguage names to refer to the steps.
Next, we state and prove some basic properties of
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
.

Proposition 1
(Strong Normalization)

There is no infinite reduction
t_{0}\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}t_{1}\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}\ldots
.

Proof

Observe that a reduction step

\mathtt{C}\langle(\lambda^{\ell}x.t)\bar{s}\rangle\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}\mathtt{C}\langle t\{x:=\bar{s}\}\rangle

decreases the number of lambdas in a term by exactly 1,
because substitution is
linear
,
i.e.
the term
t\{x:=[s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}]\}

uses
s_{i}
exactly once for all
i=1..n
.

Note:

this is an adaptation of
[
8
, Theorem 4.1]
.

The substitution operator may be extended to work on lists,
by defining

[t_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}\{x:=\bar{s}\}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}[t_{i}\{x:=\bar{s}_{i}\}]_{i=1}^{n}

where
(\bar{s}_{1},\ldots,\bar{s}_{n})
is a partition of
\bar{s}

such that
\mathtt{T}_{x}(t_{i})=\mathtt{T}(\bar{s}_{i})
for all
i=1..n
.

Lemma 2
(Substitution Lemma)

Let
x\neq y
and
x\not\in\mathsf{fv}({\bar{u}})
.
If
(\bar{u}_{1},\bar{u}_{2})
is a partition of
\bar{u}

then

t\{x:=\bar{s}\}\{y:=\bar{u}\}=t\{y:=\bar{u}_{1}\}\{x:=\bar{s}\{y:=\bar{u}_{2}\}\}
,
provided that both sides of the equation are defined.
Note: there exists a list
\bar{u}
that makes the left-hand side defined
if and only if there exist lists
\bar{u}_{1},\bar{u}_{2}
that make the
right-hand side defined.

Proof

\clubsuit

By induction on
t
.

Proposition 2
(Permutation)

If
t_{0}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell_{1}}_{\texttt{{\#}}}}t_{1}

and
t_{0}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell_{2}}_{\texttt{{\#}}}}t_{2}

are different steps, then there exists a term
t_{3}\in\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
such that

t_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell_{2}}_{\texttt{{\#}}}}t_{3}
and
t_{2}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell_{1}}_{\texttt{{\#}}}}t_{3}
.

Proof

\clubsuit

By exhaustive case analysis of permutation diagrams.
Two representative cases are depicted below.
The proof uses the
Substitution Lemma
(Lem.
2
).

\textstyle{(\lambda^{\ell}x.(\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.u)\bar{r})\bar{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ell^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\ell}
\textstyle{((\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.u)\bar{r})\{x:=\bar{s}\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ell^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces(\lambda^{\ell}x.u\{y:=\bar{r}\})\bar{s}}
\scriptstyle{\ell}
\textstyle{u\{y:=\bar{r}\}\{x:=\bar{s}\}}

\textstyle{(\lambda^{\ell}x.t)[\bar{s}_{1},(\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.u)\bar{r},\bar{s}_{2}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ell^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\ell}
\textstyle{t\{x:=[\bar{s}_{1},(\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.u)\bar{r},\bar{s}_{2}]\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ell^{\prime}}
\textstyle{(\lambda^{\ell}x.t)[\bar{s}_{1},u\{y:=\bar{r}\},\bar{s}_{2}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ell}
\textstyle{t\{x:=[\bar{s}_{1},u\{y:=\bar{r}\},\bar{s}_{2}]\}}

As a consequence of
Prop.
2
,
reduction is subcommutative,

i.e.

(\mathrel{\leftarrow_{\texttt{{\#}}}}\circ\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}})\ \subseteq(\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}^{=}\circ\mathrel{\leftarrow_{\texttt{{\#}}}}^{=})

where

\mathrel{\leftarrow_{\texttt{{\#}}}}
denotes
(\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}})^{-1}

and
R^{=}
denotes the reflexive closure of
R
.
Moreover, it is well-known that subcommutativity implies
confluence
,

i.e.

(\mathrel{\leftarrow_{\texttt{{\#}}}}^{*}\circ\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}^{*})\ \subseteq(\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}^{*}\circ\mathrel{\leftarrow_{\texttt{{\#}}}}^{*})
;
see
[
33
, Prop. 1.1.10]
for a proof of this fact.

Proposition 3
(Orthogonality)

\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
is an orthogonal axiomatic rewrite system.

Proof

Let
R:t\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}s
and
S:t\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}u
.
Define the set of residuals
R/S
as the set of steps starting
on
u
that have the same label as
R
.
Note that
R/S
is empty if
R=S
,
and it is a singleton if
R\neq S
, since terms are correct so their lambdas
are uniquely labeled.
Then it is immediate to observe that axioms
Autoerasure
and
Finite Residuals
hold.
The
Finite Developments
axiom
is a consequence of
Strong Normalization
(Prop.
1
).
The
Semantic Orthogonality
axiom is a consequence of
Permutation
(Prop.
2
).

For instance, in the reduction graph of Ex.
1
,

ST/RS^{\prime}=T^{\prime}
,

S\sqcup R=SR^{\prime}
,
and
SR^{\prime}T^{\prime}\equiv RS^{\prime}T^{\prime}
.
Observe that in Ex.
1
there is no
duplication or erasure of steps. This is a general phenomenon.
Indeed,
Permutation
(Prop.
2
) ensures that all
non-trivial permutation diagrams are closed with exactly one step on each side.

Let us write
\mathbb{D}^{\texttt{{\#}}}(t)
for the set of derivations of
t
in the
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
-calculus,
modulo permutation equivalence.
As a consequence of

Orthogonality
(Prop.
3
)
and axiomatic results
[
28
]
,
the set
\mathbb{D}^{\texttt{{\#}}}(t)
is an upper semilattice.
Actually, we show that moreover the space
\mathbb{D}^{\texttt{{\#}}}(t)

is a
distributive lattice
.
To prove this, let us start by mentioning
the property that we call
Full Stability
.
This is a strong version of stability in the sense of Lévy
[
26
]
.
It means that steps are created in an essentially unique way.
In what follows, we write
\mathsf{lab}(R)
for the label of a step,
and
\mathsf{labs}(R_{1}\ldots R_{n})=\{\mathsf{lab}(R_{i})\ |\ 1\leq i\leq n\}
for the set of
labels of a derivation.

Lemma 3
(Full Stability)

Let
\rho,\sigma
be coinitial derivations
with disjoint labels, i.e.
\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\cap\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)=\varnothing
.
Let
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
be steps such that

T_{3}=T_{1}/(\sigma/\rho)=T_{2}/(\rho/\sigma)
.
Then there is a step
T_{0}
such that
T_{1}=T_{0}/\rho

and
T_{2}=T_{0}/\sigma
.

Proof

\clubsuit

The proof is easily reduced to a
Basic Stability
result:
a particular case of
Full Stability
when
\rho
and
\sigma

consist of single steps.

Basic Stability
is proved by exhaustive case analysis.

Proposition 4

\mathbb{D}^{\texttt{{\#}}}(t)
is a lattice.

Proof

\clubsuit

The missing components are the
top
and the
meet
.
The top element is given by
\top:={[\rho]}
where
\rho:t\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}^{*}s

is a derivation to normal form, which exists by
Strong Normalization
(Prop.
1
).
The meet of
\{{[\rho]},{[\sigma]}\}
is constructed using

Full Stability
(Lem.
15
).
If
\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\cap\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)=\varnothing
,
define
(\rho\sqcap\sigma):=\epsilon
.
Otherwise, the stability result ensures that there is a step

R
coinitial to
\rho
and
\sigma

such that
\mathsf{lab}(R)\in\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\cap\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)
.
Let
R
be one such step,
and, recursively, define

(\rho\sqcap\sigma):=R((\rho/R)\sqcap(\sigma/R))
.
It can be checked that recursion terminates,
because
\mathsf{labs}(\rho/R)\subset\mathsf{labs}(\rho)
is a strict
inclusion.
Moreover,
\rho\sqcap\sigma

is the greatest lower bound of
\{\rho,\sigma\}
,
up to permutation equivalence.

For instance, in Ex.
1

we have that

ST\sqcap R=\epsilon
,

ST\sqcap RS^{\prime}=S
,
and
ST\sqcap RS^{\prime}T^{\prime}=ST
.

Proposition 5

There is a monomorphism of lattices
\mathbb{D}^{\texttt{{\#}}}(t)\to\mathcal{P}(X)

for some set
X
.
The lattice
(\mathcal{P}(X),\subseteq,\varnothing,\cup,X,\cap)
consists of the subsets of
X
, ordered by inclusion.

Proof

\clubsuit

The morphism is the function
\mathsf{labs}
, mapping each derivation to its set of labels.

This means that a derivation in
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
is characterized, up to permutation equivalence,
by the set of labels of its steps.
Since
\mathcal{P}(X)
is a distributive lattice, in particular we have:

Corollary 1

\mathbb{D}^{\texttt{{\#}}}(t)
is a distributive lattice.

4
Simulation of the
\lambda
-Calculus in the
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
-Calculus

In this section we establish a precise relationship between derivations in
the
\lambda
-calculus and derivations in
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
.
To begin, we need a way to relate
\lambda
-terms and correct terms (
\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
):

Definition 6
(Refinement)

A correct term
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}

refines
a
\lambda
-term
t
,
written
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
, according to the following inductive definition:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 12.80557pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=25.61115pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\texttt{{r-var}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x^{\mathcal{A}}\mathrel{\ltimes}x$}}}}\end{array}\hskip 14.22636pt\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 17.91667pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=53.63893pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\texttt{{r-lam}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\lambda^{\ell}x.t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\lambda x.t$}}}}\end{array}\hskip 14.22636pt\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t\hskip 14.22636pts^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\ltimes}s\text{ for all $i=1..n$}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=121.71141pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\texttt{{r-app}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 36.09581pt\hbox{$\displaystyle t^{\prime}[s^{\prime}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}\mathrel{\ltimes}ts$}}}}\end{array}

A
\lambda
-term may have many refinements.
For example, the following terms refine
(\lambda x.xx)y
:

(\lambda^{1}x.x^{[\,]\overset{2}{\to}\alpha^{3}}[\,])[y^{[\,]\overset{2}{\to}\alpha^{3}}]\hskip 14.22636pt(\lambda^{1}x.x^{[\alpha^{2}]\overset{3}{\to}\beta^{4}}[x^{\alpha^{2}}])[y^{[\alpha^{2}]\overset{3}{\to}\beta^{4}},y^{\alpha^{2}}]

(\lambda^{1}x.x^{[\alpha^{2},\beta^{3}]\overset{4}{\to}\gamma^{5}}[x^{\alpha^{2}},x^{\beta^{3}}])[y^{[\alpha^{2},\beta^{3}]\overset{4}{\to}\gamma^{5}},y^{\alpha^{2}},y^{\beta^{3}}]

The refinement relation establishes a relation of
simulation
between the
\lambda
-calculus and
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
.

Proposition 6
(Simulation)

Let
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
. Then:

1.

If
t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}s
, there exists
s^{\prime}

such that
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}^{*}s^{\prime}

and
s^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}s
.

2.

If
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}s^{\prime}
, there exist
s
and
s^{\prime\prime}

such that

t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}s
,

s^{\prime}\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}^{*}s^{\prime\prime}
,
and
s^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}s
.

Proof

\clubsuit

By case analysis. The proof is constructive.
Moreover, in item
1
,
the derivation
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}^{*}s^{\prime}
is shown to be a
multistep
,
i.e.
the complete
development of a set
\{R_{1},\ldots,R_{n}\}
.

The following example illustrates that a
\beta
-step in the
\lambda
-calculus may be simulated by zero,
one, or possibly many steps in
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
, depending on the refinement chosen.

Example 2

The following are simulations of the step
x\,((\lambda x.x)y)\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}x\,y

using
\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}
-steps:

\textstyle{x\,((\lambda x.x)y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\rtimes}
\textstyle{x\,y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\rtimes}
\textstyle{x^{[]\overset{1}{\to}\alpha^{2}}\,[]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{x^{[]\overset{1}{\to}\alpha^{2}}\,[]}

\textstyle{x\,((\lambda x.x)y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\rtimes}
\textstyle{x\,y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\rtimes}
\textstyle{x^{[\alpha^{1}]\overset{2}{\to}\beta^{3}}\,[(\lambda^{4}x.x^{\alpha^{1}})[y^{\alpha^{1}}]]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
#
\textstyle{x^{[\alpha^{1}]\overset{2}{\to}\beta^{3}}\,[y^{\alpha^{1}}]}

\textstyle{x\,((\lambda x.x)y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\rtimes}
\textstyle{x\,y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\rtimes}
\textstyle{x^{[\alpha^{1},\beta^{2}]\overset{3}{\to}\gamma^{4}}\,[(\lambda^{5}x.x^{\alpha^{1}})[y^{\alpha^{1}}],(\lambda^{6}x.x^{\beta^{2}})[y^{\beta^{2}}]]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
#
\textstyle{x^{[\alpha^{1},\beta^{2}]\overset{3}{\to}\gamma^{4}}\,[y^{\alpha^{1}},y^{\beta^{2}}]}

The next result relates typability and normalization.
This is an adaptation of existing results from
non-idempotent intersection types,
e.g.

[
8
, Lemma 5.1]
.
Recall that a

head normal form
is a term of the form

\lambda x_{1}.\ldots\lambda x_{n}.y\,t_{1}\,\ldots\,t_{m}
.

Proposition 7
(Typability characterizes head normalization)

The following are equivalent:

1.

There exists
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
such that
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
.

2.

There exists a head normal form
s
such that
t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}^{*}s
.

Proof

\clubsuit

The implication
(1\implies 2)

relies on
Simulation
(Prop.
6
).
The implication
(2\implies 1)

relies on the fact that head normal forms are typable,
plus an auxiliary result of
Subject Expansion
.

The first item of
Simulation
(Prop.
6
)
ensures that every step
t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}s
can be simulated
in
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
starting from a term
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
.
Actually, a finer relationship can be established between the
derivation spaces
\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}(t)
and
\mathbb{D}^{\texttt{{\#}}}(t^{\prime})
.
For this, we introduce the notion of
simulation residual
.

Definition 7
(Simulation residuals)

Let
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
and let
R:t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}s
be a step.
The constructive proof of
Simulation
(Prop.
6
)
associates the
\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}
-step
R
to a possibly empty set of
\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}
-steps

\{R_{1},\ldots,R_{n}\}
all of which start from
t^{\prime}
.
We write
R/t^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\{R_{1},\ldots,R_{n}\}
,
and we call
R_{1},\ldots,R_{n}
the
simulation residuals of
R
after
t^{\prime}
.
All the complete developments of
R/t^{\prime}
have a common target,
which we denote by
t^{\prime}/R
,
called the
simulation residual of
t^{\prime}
after
R
.

Recall that, by abuse of notation,
R/t^{\prime}
stands
for some complete development of the set
R/t^{\prime}
.
By
Simulation
(Prop.
6
),
the following diagram always holds given
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}s
:

\textstyle{t\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{\rtimes}
\textstyle{s\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\rtimes}
\textstyle{t^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
#
\scriptstyle{R/t^{\prime}}
\textstyle{t^{\prime}/R}

Example 3
(Simulation residuals)

Let
R:x\,((\lambda x.x)y)\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}x\,y

and consider the terms:

t^{\prime}_{0}=(x^{[\alpha^{1},\beta^{2}]\overset{3}{\to}\gamma^{4}}\,[(\lambda^{5}x.x^{\alpha^{1}})[y^{\alpha^{1}}],(\lambda^{6}x.x^{\beta^{2}})[y^{\beta^{2}}]])

t^{\prime}_{1}=x^{[\alpha^{1},\beta^{2}]\overset{3}{\to}\gamma^{4}}\,[y^{\alpha^{1}},(\lambda^{6}x.x^{\beta^{2}})[y^{\beta^{2}}]

t^{\prime}_{2}=x^{[\alpha^{1},\beta^{2}]\overset{3}{\to}\gamma^{4}}\,[(\lambda^{5}x.x^{\alpha^{1}})[y^{\alpha^{1}}],y^{\beta^{2}}]

t^{\prime}_{3}=x^{[\alpha^{1},\beta^{2}]\overset{3}{\to}\gamma^{4}}\,[y^{\alpha^{1}},y^{\beta^{2}}]

Then
t^{\prime}_{0}/R=t^{\prime}_{3}

and
R/t^{\prime}_{0}=\{R_{1},R_{2}\}
, where

R_{1}:t^{\prime}_{0}\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}t^{\prime}_{1}

and

R_{2}:t^{\prime}_{0}\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}t^{\prime}_{2}
.

The notion of simulation residual can be extended for many-step derivations.

Definition 8
(Simulation residuals of/after derivations)

If
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
and
\rho:t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}^{*}s
is a derivation,
then
\rho/t^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}/\rho
are defined as follows by induction on
\rho
:

\begin{array}[]{c@{\hspace{.5cm}}c@{\hspace{.5cm}}c@{\hspace{.5cm}}c}\epsilon/t^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\epsilon\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&R\sigma/t^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(R/t^{\prime})(\sigma/(t^{\prime}/R))\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&t^{\prime}/\epsilon\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}t^{\prime}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&t^{\prime}/R\sigma\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(t^{\prime}/R)/\sigma\\
\end{array}

It is then easy to check that
\rho/t^{\prime}:t^{\prime}\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}^{*}t^{\prime}/\rho
and
t^{\prime}/\rho\mathrel{\ltimes}s
, by induction on
\rho
.
Moreover, simulation residuals are well-defined modulo permutation equivalence:

Proposition 8
(Compatibility)

If
\rho\equiv\sigma

and
t\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathsf{src}(\rho)

then

\rho/t\equiv\sigma/t

and
t/\rho=t/\sigma
.

Proof

\clubsuit

By case analysis,
studying how permutation diagrams in the
\lambda
-calculus
are transported to permutation diagrams in
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}

via simulation.

The following result resembles the usual Cube Lemma
[
4
, Lemma 12.2.6]
:

Lemma 4
(Cube)

If
t\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathsf{src}(\rho)=\mathsf{src}(\sigma)
, then
(\rho/\sigma)/(t/\sigma)\equiv(\rho/t)/(\sigma/t)
.

Proof

\clubsuit

By induction on
\rho
and
\sigma
,
relying on an auxiliary result, the
Basic Cube Lemma
,
when
\rho
and
\sigma
are single steps,
proved by exhaustive case analysis.

As a result,

(\rho\sqcup\sigma)/t=\rho(\sigma/\rho)/t=(\rho/t)((\sigma/\rho)/(t/\rho))\equiv(\rho/t)((\sigma/t)/(\sigma/\rho))=(\rho/t)\sqcup(\sigma/t)
.
Moreover, if
\rho\sqsubseteq\sigma

then
\rho\tau\equiv\sigma
for some
\tau
.
So we have that
\rho/t\sqsubseteq(\rho/t)(\tau/(t/\rho))=\rho\tau/t\equiv\sigma/t

by
Compatibility
(Prop.
8
).
Hence we may formulate a stronger simulation result:

Corollary 2
(Algebraic Simulation)

Let
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
.
Then the mapping
\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}(t)\to\mathbb{D}^{\texttt{{\#}}}(t^{\prime})

given by
{[\rho]}\mapsto{[\rho/t^{\prime}]}
is a morphism of upper
semilattices.

Example 4

Let
I=\lambda x.x

and
\Delta=(\lambda^{5}x.x^{\alpha^{2}})[z^{\alpha^{2}}]

and let
\hat{y}=y^{[\alpha^{2}]\overset{3}{\to}[\,]\overset{4}{\to}\beta^{5}}
.
The refinement
t^{\prime}:=(\lambda^{1}x.\hat{y}[x^{\alpha^{2}}][\,])[\Delta]\mathrel{\ltimes}(\lambda x.yxx)(Iz)

induces a morphism between the upper semilattices represented by the following reduction graphs:

\textstyle{(\lambda x.yxx)(Iz)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{1}}
\scriptstyle{S}
\textstyle{y(Iz)(Iz)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{11}}
\scriptstyle{S_{21}}
\textstyle{y(Iz)z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{12}}
\textstyle{(\lambda x.yxx)z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{2}}
\textstyle{yz(Iz)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{22}}
\textstyle{yzz}
\textstyle{(\lambda^{1}x.\hat{y}[x^{\alpha^{2}}][\,])[\Delta]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{S^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\hat{y}[\Delta][\,]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{\hskip-28.45274pt(\lambda^{1}x.\hat{y}[x^{\alpha^{2}}][\,])[z^{\alpha^{2}}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R^{\prime}_{2}}
\textstyle{\hat{y}[z^{\alpha^{2}}][\,]}

For example

(R_{1}\sqcup S)/t^{\prime}=(R_{1}S_{11}S_{22})/t^{\prime}=R^{\prime}_{1}S^{\prime}_{1}=R^{\prime}_{1}\sqcup S^{\prime}=R_{1}/t^{\prime}\sqcup S/t^{\prime}
.
Note that the step
S_{22}
is erased by the simulation:
S_{22}/(\hat{y}[z^{\alpha^{2}}][\,])=\varnothing
.
Intuitively,
S_{22}
is “garbage” with respect to the refinement
\hat{y}[z^{\alpha^{2}}][\,]
,
because it lies inside an
untyped
argument.

5
Factoring Derivation Spaces

In this section we prove that the upper semilattice
\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}(t)

may be factorized using a variant of the Grothendieck construction.
We start by formally defining the notion of
garbage
.

Definition 9
(Garbage)

Let
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
.
A derivation
\rho:t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}^{*}s
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage

if
\rho/t^{\prime}=\epsilon
.

The informal idea is that each refinement
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t

specifies that some subterms of
t
are “useless”.
A subterm
u
is useless if it lies inside the argument
of an application
s(...u...)

in such a way that the argument is not typed,
i.e.
the refinement is
of the form
s^{\prime}[\,]\mathrel{\ltimes}s(...u...)
.
A single step
R
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage if the pattern of the contracted
redex lies inside a useless subterm.
A sequence of steps
R_{1}R_{2}\ldots R_{n}

is
t^{\prime}
-garbage if
R_{1}
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage,

R_{2}
is
(t^{\prime}/R_{1})
-garbage,

\ldots
,

R_{i}
is
(t^{\prime}/R_{1}\ldots R_{i-1})
-garbage,

\ldots
,
and so on.

Usually we say that
\rho
is just
garbage
, when
t^{\prime}
is clear from the context.
For instance, in Ex.
4
,

S_{21}
is garbage, since
S_{21}/(\hat{y}[\Delta][\,])=\epsilon
.
Similarly,
S_{22}
is garbage, since
S_{22}/(\hat{y}[z^{\alpha^{2}}][\,])=\epsilon
.
On the other hand,
R_{1}S_{21}
is not garbage, since

R_{1}S_{21}/((\lambda^{1}x.\hat{y}[x^{\alpha^{2}}][\,])[\Delta])=R^{\prime}_{1}\neq\epsilon
.
For each
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
, the set of
t^{\prime}
-garbage derivations forms an
ideal
of the upper semilattice
\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}(t)
.
More precisely:

Proposition 9
(Properties of garbage)

Let
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
. Then:

1.

If
\rho
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage and
\sigma\sqsubseteq\rho
, then
\sigma
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage.

2.

The composition
\rho\sigma
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage if and only if
\rho
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage and
\sigma
is
(t^{\prime}/\rho)
-garbage.

3.

If
\rho
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage then
\rho/\sigma
is
(t^{\prime}/\sigma)
-garbage.

4.

The join
\rho\sqcup\sigma
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage if and only if
\rho
and
\sigma
are
t^{\prime}
-garbage.

Proof

\clubsuit

The proof is easy using Prop.
8
and Lem.
4
.

Our aim is to show that given
\rho:t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}^{*}s
and
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
,
there is a unique way of decomposing
\rho
as
\sigma\tau
,
where
\tau
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage and
\sigma
“has no
t^{\prime}
-garbage”.
Garbage is well-defined modulo permutation equivalence,

i.e.
given
\rho\equiv\sigma
, we have that
\rho
is garbage if and only if
\sigma
is garbage.
In contrast, it is not immediate to give a well-defined notion of “having no garbage”.
For example, in Ex.
4
,

SR_{2}
has no garbage steps, so it appears to have no garbage;
however, it is permutation equivalent to
R_{1}S_{11}S_{22}
, which
does contain a garbage step (
S_{22}
).
The following definition seems to capture the right notion of having no garbage:

Definition 10
(Garbage-free derivation)

Let
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
. A derivation
\rho:t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}^{*}s

is
t^{\prime}
-garbage-free if
for any derivation
\sigma

such that
\sigma\sqsubseteq\rho

and
\rho/\sigma
is
(t^{\prime}/\sigma)
-garbage,
then
\rho/\sigma=\epsilon
.

Again, we omit the
t^{\prime}
if clear from the context.
Going back to Ex.
4
,
the derivation
SR_{2}
is not garbage-free,
because
R_{1}S_{11}\sqsubseteq SR_{2}

and
SR_{2}/R_{1}S_{11}=S_{22}
is garbage but non-empty.
Note that Def.
10

is defined in terms of the prefix order (
\sqsubseteq
), so:

Remark 3

If
\rho\equiv\sigma
,
then
\rho
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage-free if and only if
\sigma
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage-free.

Next, we define an effective procedure (
sieving
)
to erase all the garbage from a derivation.
The idea is that if
\rho:t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}^{\star}s
is a derivation
in the
\lambda
-calculus and
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
is any refinement,
we may constructively build a
t^{\prime}
-garbage-free derivation
(\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime}):t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}^{\star}u

by erasing all the
t^{\prime}
-garbage from
\rho
.
Our goal will then be to show that
\rho\equiv(\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime})\sigma

where
\sigma
is garbage.

Definition 11
(Sieving)

Let
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
and
\rho:t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}^{\star}s
.
A step
R
is
coarse for
(\rho,t^{\prime})
if

R\sqsubseteq\rho
and
R/t^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
.
The
sieve of
\rho
with respect to
t^{\prime}
,
written
\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime}
,
is defined as follows.

•

If there are no coarse steps for
(\rho,t^{\prime})
,
then
(\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\epsilon
.

•

If there is a coarse step for
(\rho,t^{\prime})
,
then
(\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}R_{0}((\rho/R_{0})\Downarrow(t^{\prime}/R_{0}))

where
R_{0}
is the leftmost such step.

Lemma 5

The sieving operation
\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime}
is well-defined.

Proof

\clubsuit

To see that recursion terminates, consider the measure
M
given by
M(\rho,t^{\prime}):=\#\mathsf{labs}(\rho/t^{\prime})
, and note that
M(\rho,t^{\prime})>M(\rho/R_{0},t^{\prime}/R_{0})
.

For example, in Ex.
4
,
we have that

S\Downarrow t^{\prime}=S

and

SR_{2}\Downarrow t^{\prime}=R_{1}S_{11}
.

Proposition 10
(Properties of sieving)

Let
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
and
\rho:t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}^{*}s
. Then:

1.

\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime}
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage-free and
\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\rho
.

2.

\rho/(\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime})
is
(t^{\prime}/(\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime}))
-garbage.

3.

\rho
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage if and only if
\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime}=\epsilon
.

4.

\rho
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage-free if and only if
\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime}\equiv\rho
.

Proof

\clubsuit

By induction on the length of
\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime}
, using various technical lemmas.

As a consequence of the definition of the sieving construction and its properties,
given any derivation
\rho:t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}^{*}s

and any refinement
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
,
we can always write
\rho
, modulo permutation equivalence, as of the form

\rho\equiv\sigma\tau

in such a way that
\sigma
is garbage-free and
\tau
is garbage.
To prove this take
\sigma:=\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime}

and
\tau:=\rho/(\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime})
,
and note that
\sigma
is garbage-free by item 1. of Prop.
10
,

\tau
is garbage by item 2. of Prop.
10
,
and
\rho\equiv\sigma(\rho/\sigma)=\sigma\tau

because
\sigma\sqsubseteq\rho
by item 1. of Prop.
10
.

In the following we give a stronger version of this result.
The
Factorization
theorem below (Thm.
5.1
)
states that this decomposition is actually an isomorphism of upper semilattices.
This means, on one hand, that given any derivation
\rho:t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}^{*}s

and any refinement
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t

there is a
unique
way to factor
\rho
as of the form

\rho\equiv\sigma\tau
where
\sigma
is garbage-free
and
\tau
is garbage.
On the other hand, it means that the decomposition

\rho\mapsto(\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime},\rho/(\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime}))

mapping each derivation to a of a garbage-free plus a garbage derivation
is
functorial
.
This means, essentially, that the set of pairs
(\sigma,\tau)
such that
\sigma
is garbage-free
and
\tau
is garbage can be given the structure of an upper semilattice
in such a way that:

•

If
\rho\mapsto(\sigma,\tau)

and
\rho^{\prime}\mapsto(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})

then
\rho\sqsubseteq\rho^{\prime}\iff(\sigma,\tau)\leq(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
.

•

If
\rho\mapsto(\sigma,\tau)

and
\rho^{\prime}\mapsto(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})

then
(\rho\sqcup\rho^{\prime})\mapsto(\sigma,\tau)\lor(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
.

The upper semilattice structure of the set of pairs
(\sigma,\tau)

is given using a variant of the Grothendieck construction:

Definition 12
(Grothendieck construction for partially ordered sets)

Let
A
be a poset, and let
B:A\to\mathsf{Poset}
be a mapping associating
each object
a\in A
to a poset
B(a)
.
Suppose moreover that
B
is a
lax 2-functor
.
More precisely, for each
a\leq b
in
A
,
the function
B(a\hookrightarrow b):B(a)\to B(b)

is monotonic and such that:

1.

B(a\hookrightarrow a)=\mathsf{id}_{B(a)}
for all
a\in A
,

2.

B((b\hookrightarrow c)\circ(a\hookrightarrow b))\leq B(b\hookrightarrow c)\circ B(a\hookrightarrow b)
for all
a\leq b\leq c
in
A
.

The
Grothendieck construction

\int_{A}{B}

is defined as the poset
given by the set of objects

\{(a,b)\ |\ a\in A,\ b\in B(a)\}

and such that
(a,b)\leq(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})

is declared to hold if and only if
A\leq a^{\prime}\text{ and }B(a\hookrightarrow a^{\prime})(b)\leq b^{\prime}
.

The following proposition states that garbage-free derivations form a finite lattice,
while garbage derivations form an upper semilattice.

Proposition 11
(Garbage-free and garbage semilattices)

Let
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
.

1.

The set

F=\{{[\rho]}\ |\ \mathsf{src}(\rho)=t\text{ and }\rho\text{ is $t^{\prime}$-garbage-free}\}

of
t^{\prime}
-garbage-free derivations forms a finite lattice

\mathbb{F}(t^{\prime},t)=(F,\unlhd,\bot,\triangledown,\top,\!\vartriangle\!)
, with:

•

Partial order:
{[\rho]}\unlhd{[\sigma]}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{\iff}\rho/\sigma\text{ is $(t^{\prime}/\sigma)$-garbage}
.

•

Bottom:
\bot:={[\epsilon]}
.

•

Join:
{[\rho]}\triangledown{[\sigma]}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}{[(\rho\sqcup\sigma)\Downarrow t^{\prime}]}
.

•

Top:
\top
, defined as the join of all the
{[\tau]}
such that
\tau
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage-free.

•

Meet:
{[\rho]}\!\vartriangle\!{[\sigma]}
,
defined as the join of all the
{[\tau]}
such that
{[\tau]}\unlhd{[\rho]}
and
{[\tau]}\unlhd{[\sigma]}
.

2.

The set
G=\{{[\rho]}\ |\ \mathsf{src}(\rho)=t\text{ and }\rho\text{ is $t^{\prime}$-garbage}\}

of
t^{\prime}
-garbage derivations forms an upper semilattice
\mathbb{G}(t^{\prime},t)=(G,\sqsubseteq,\bot,\sqcup)
,
with the structure inherited from
\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}(t)
.

Proof

\clubsuit

The proof relies on the properties of garbage and sieving (Prop.
9
, Prop.
10
).

Suppose that
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
,
and let
\mathcal{F}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathbb{F}(t^{\prime},t)
denote the lattice
of
t^{\prime}
-garbage-free derivations.
Let
\mathcal{G}:\mathcal{F}\to\mathsf{Poset}
be the lax 2-functor

\mathcal{G}({[\rho]})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathbb{G}(t^{\prime}/\rho,\mathsf{tgt}(\rho))

with the following action on morphisms:

\begin{array}[]{rrlll}\mathcal{G}({[\rho]}\hookrightarrow{[\sigma]})&:&\mathcal{G}({[\rho]})&\to&\mathcal{G}({[\sigma]})\\
&&{[\alpha]}&\mapsto&{[\rho\alpha/\sigma]}\end{array}

Using the previous proposition (Prop.
11
)
it can be checked that
\mathcal{G}
is indeed a lax 2-functor, and that
the Grothendieck construction
\int_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathcal{G}}
forms an upper semilattice. The join is given by

(a,b)\lor(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})=(a\triangledown a^{\prime},\mathcal{G}(a\hookrightarrow a\triangledown a^{\prime})(b)\sqcup\mathcal{G}(a^{\prime}\hookrightarrow a\triangledown a^{\prime})(b^{\prime}))
.
Finally we can state the main theorem:

Theorem 5.1
(Factorization)

The following maps form an isomorphism of upper semilattices:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}(t)&\to&\int_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathcal{G}}\\
{[\rho]}&\mapsto&({[\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime}]},{[\rho/(\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime})]}\\
\end{array}\hskip 14.22636pt\begin{array}[]{rcl}\int_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathcal{G}}&\to&\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}(t)\\
({[\rho]},{[\sigma]})&\mapsto&{[\rho\sigma]}\\
\end{array}

Proof

\clubsuit

The proof consists in checking that both maps are morphisms of upper semilattices and
that they are mutual inverses, resorting to Prop.
9
and Prop.
10
.

Example 5

Let
t=(\lambda x.yxx)(Iz)

and
t^{\prime}
be as in Ex.
4
.
The upper semilattice
\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}(t)
can be factorized as
\int_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathcal{G}}

as follows. Here posets are represented by their Hasse diagrams:
