&=&(\mathsf{labs}(S)\cup\mathsf{labs}(\sigma))\setminus\{\mathsf{lab}(R)\}&\text{since $\mathsf{lab}(R)\neq\mathsf{lab}(S)$}\\
&=&\mathsf{labs}(S\sigma)\setminus\{\mathsf{lab}(R)\}\\
&=&\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\setminus\{\mathsf{lab}(R)\}\\
\end{array}

which completes the claim.
To see that
\mathsf{labs}(\rho/\sigma)=\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\setminus\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)

for an arbitrary derivation
\sigma
,
proceed by induction on
\sigma
.
If
\sigma
is empty it is trivial, so
consider the case in which
\sigma=R\tau
.

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\mathsf{labs}(\rho/R\tau)&=&\mathsf{labs}((\rho/R)/\tau)\\
&=&\mathsf{labs}(\rho/R)\setminus\mathsf{labs}(\tau)&\text{ by {i.h.} }\\
&=&(\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\setminus\{\mathsf{lab}(R)\})\setminus\mathsf{labs}(\tau)&\text{ by the previous claim}\\
&=&\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\setminus(\{\mathsf{lab}(R)\}\cup\mathsf{labs}(\tau))\\
&=&\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\setminus\mathsf{labs}(R\tau)\\
\end{array}

Proposition 12
(Prefixes as subsets)

Let
\rho,\sigma
be coinitial derivations in the distributive lambda-calculus.
Then

\rho\sqsubseteq\sigma
if and only if
\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\subseteq\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)
.

Proof

By induction on
\rho
.
The base case is immediate since
\epsilon\sqsubseteq\sigma

and
\varnothing\subseteq\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)
both hold.
So let
\rho=T\tau
. First note that
the following equivalence holds:

T\tau\sqsubseteq\sigma\hskip 14.22636pt\iff\hskip 14.22636ptT\sqsubseteq\sigma\ \land\ \tau\sqsubseteq\sigma/T

(1)

Indeed:

(\Rightarrow)

Suppose that
T\tau\sqsubseteq\sigma
.
Then, on one hand,
T\sqsubseteq T\tau\sqsubseteq\sigma
.
On the other hand, projection is monotonic, so

\tau=T\tau/T\sqsubseteq\sigma/T
.

(\Leftarrow)

Since
\tau\sqsubseteq\sigma/T

we have that
T\tau\sqsubseteq T(\sigma/T)\equiv\sigma(T/\sigma)=\sigma

since
T/\sigma=\epsilon
.

So we have that:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}T\tau\sqsubseteq\sigma&\iff&T\sqsubseteq\sigma\ \land\ \tau\sqsubseteq\sigma/T&\text{ by (\ref{eqn:prefixes_as_subsets:1})}\\
&\iff&\mathsf{lab}(T)\in\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)\ \land\ \tau\sqsubseteq\sigma/T&\text{ by Lem.~\ref{lemma:characterization_of_belonging}}\\
&\iff&\mathsf{lab}(T)\in\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)\ \land\ \mathsf{labs}(\tau)\subseteq\mathsf{labs}(\sigma/T)&\text{ by {i.h.} }\\
&\iff&\mathsf{lab}(T)\in\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)\ \land\ \mathsf{labs}(\tau)\subseteq\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)\setminus\{\mathsf{lab}(T)\}&\text{ by Lem.~\ref{lemma:names_after_projection_along_a_step}}\\
&\iff&\mathsf{labs}(T\tau)\subseteq\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)\end{array}

To justify the very last equivalence, the
(\Rightarrow)
direction is immediate.
For the
(\Leftarrow)
direction,
the difficulty is ensuring that

\mathsf{labs}(\tau)\subseteq\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)\setminus\{\mathsf{lab}(T)\}

from the fact that

\mathsf{labs}(T\tau)\subseteq\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)
.
To see this it suffices to observe that by Lem.
17
,

\mathsf{labs}(T\tau)

is the
disjoint
union

\mathsf{labs}(T)\uplus\mathsf{labs}(\tau)
,
which means that

\mathsf{lab}(T)\not\in\mathsf{labs}(\tau)
.

As an easy consequence:

Corollary 4
(Permutation equivalence in terms of labels)

Let
\rho,\sigma
be coinitial derivations in
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
.
Then

\rho\equiv\sigma
if and only if
\mathsf{labs}(\rho)=\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)
.

Lemma 19
(Projections are decreasing)

Let
R\in\rho
. Then
|\rho|=1+|\rho/R|
.

Proof

Observe that
R\sqsubseteq\rho
by Lem.
16
.
So
\rho\equiv R(\rho/R)
, which gives us that:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}|\rho|&=&\#\mathsf{labs}(\rho)&\text{ by Coro.~\ref{coro:length_of_derivation_is_number_of_distinct_names}}\\
&=&\#\mathsf{labs}(R(\rho/R))&\text{ by Coro.~\ref{coro:permutation_equivalence_in_terms_of_names}, since $\rho\equiv R(\rho/R)$}\\
&=&\#(\mathsf{labs}(R)\uplus\mathsf{labs}(\rho/R)&\text{ by Lem.~\ref{lemma:names_concatenation_disjoint_union}}\\
&=&1+\#\mathsf{labs}(\rho/R)\\
&=&1+|\rho/R|&\text{ by Coro.~\ref{coro:length_of_derivation_is_number_of_distinct_names}}\\
\end{array}

The proof of Prop.
4
proceeds as follows.
If
\rho
and
\sigma
are derivations, we say that a step
R

is a
common
(to
\rho
and
\sigma
) whenever
R\in\rho
and
R\in\sigma
.
Define
\rho\sqcap\sigma
as follows,
by induction on the length of
\rho
:

\rho\sqcap\sigma\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\begin{cases}\epsilon&\text{if there are no common steps to $\rho$ and $\sigma$}\\
R((\rho/R)\sqcap(\sigma/R))&\text{if the step $R$ is common to $\rho$ and $\sigma$ }\\
\end{cases}

In the second case of the definition, there might be more than one
R

common to
\rho
and
\sigma
.
Any such step may be chosen and we make no further assumptions.
To see that this recursive construction is well-defined,
note that the length of
\rho/R
is lesser than the length of
\rho

by the fact that projections are decreasing (Lem.
19
).
To conclude the construction, we show that
\rho\sqcap\sigma
is an infimum,
i.e.
a greatest lower bound:

1.

Lower bound.

Let us show that
\rho\sqcap\sigma\sqsubseteq\rho

by induction on the length of
\rho
.
There are two subcases, depending on whether
there is a step common to
\rho
and
\sigma
.

If there is no common step,
then
\rho\sqcap\sigma=\epsilon

trivially verifies
\rho\sqcap\sigma\sqsubseteq\rho
.

On the other hand, if there is a common step, we have by definition that

\rho\sqcap\sigma=R((\rho/R)\sqcap(\sigma/R))

where
R
is common to
\rho
and
\sigma
.
Recall that projections are decreasing (Lem.
19
)
so
|\rho|>|\rho/R|
.
This allows us to apply the
i.h.
and conclude:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\rho\sqcap\sigma&=&R((\rho/R)\sqcap(\sigma/R))&\text{ by definition}\\
&\sqsubseteq&R(\rho/R)&\text{ since by {i.h.} $(\rho/R)\sqcap(\sigma/R)\sqsubseteq\rho/R$}\\
&\equiv&\rho(R/\rho)\\
&=&\rho&\text{ since $R\sqsubseteq\rho$ by Lem.~\ref{lemma:characterization_of_belonging}.}\\
\end{array}

Showing that
\rho\sqcap\sigma\sqsubseteq\sigma
is symmetric,
by induction on the length of
\sigma
.

2.

Greatest lower bound.

Let
\tau
be a lower bound for
\{\rho,\sigma\}
,

i.e.

\tau\sqsubseteq\rho
and
\tau\sqsubseteq\sigma
,
and let us show that
\tau\sqsubseteq\rho\sqcap\sigma
.
We proceed by induction on the length of
\rho
.
There are two subcases, depending on whether
there is a step common to
\rho
and
\sigma
.

If there is no common step,
we claim that
\tau
must be empty.
Otherwise we would have that
\tau=T\tau^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\rho

so in particular
T\sqsubseteq\rho
and
T\in\rho
by Lem.
16
.
Similarly,
T\in\sigma
so

T
is a step common to
\rho
and
\sigma
,
which is a contradiction.
We obtain that
\tau
is empty,
so trivially
\tau=\epsilon\sqsubseteq\rho\sqcap\sigma
.

On the other hand, if there is a common step, we have by definition that

\rho\sqcap\sigma=R((\rho/R)\sqcap(\sigma/R))

where
R
is common to
\rho
and
\sigma
.
Moreover, since
\tau\sqsubseteq\rho
and
\tau\sqsubseteq\sigma
,
by projecting along
R
we know that

\tau/R\sqsubseteq\rho/R
and
\tau/R\sqsubseteq\sigma/R
.
So:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\tau&\sqsubseteq&\tau(R/\tau)\\
&\equiv&R(\tau/R)\\
&\sqsubseteq&R((\rho/R)\sqcap(\sigma/R))&\text{ since by {i.h.} $\tau/R\sqsubseteq(\rho/R)\sqcap(\sigma/R)$}\\
&=&\rho\sqcap\sigma&\text{ by definition}\end{array}

Finally, observe that
the meet of
\{\rho,\sigma\}

is unique modulo permutation equivalence,
since
if
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
both
have the universal property of being a greatest lower bound,
then
\tau\sqsubseteq\tau^{\prime}

and
\tau^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\tau
.

0.A.6
Proof of Prop.
5
– The function
\mathsf{labs}
is a morphism of lattices

We need the following auxiliary result:

Lemma 20
(Disjoint derivations)

Let
\rho
and
\sigma
be coinitial derivations in
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
.
The following are equivalent:

1.

\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\cap\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)=\varnothing
.

2.

\rho\sqcap\sigma=\epsilon
.

3.

There is no step common to
\rho
and
\sigma
.

In this case we say that
\rho
and
\sigma
are disjoint.

Proof

The implication
(1\implies 2)
is immediate since if we suppose that
\rho\sqcap\sigma
is non-empty
then the first step of
\rho\sqcap\sigma
is a step
T

such that
T\in\rho
and
T\in\sigma
.
By Lem.
16
,
this means that
\mathsf{lab}(T)\in\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\cap\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)
,
contradicting the fact that
\mathsf{lab}(T)
and
\mathsf{labs}(\rho)
are disjoint.

The implication

(2\implies 3)
is immediate by the definition of
\rho\sqcap\sigma
(defined in Prop.
4
).

Let us check that the implication
(3\implies 1)
holds.
By the contrapositive, suppose that
\mathsf{labs}(\rho)
and
\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)

are not disjoint, and let us show that there is a step common to
\rho
and
\sigma
.
Since
\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\cap\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)\neq\varnothing
,
we know that the derivation
\rho
can be written as
\rho=\rho_{1}R\rho_{2}

where
\mathsf{lab}(R)\in\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)
.
Without loss of generality we may suppose
that
R
is the first step in
\rho
with that property,

i.e.
that
\mathsf{labs}(\rho_{1})\cap\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)=\varnothing
.
Moreover, let us write
\sigma
as
\sigma=\sigma_{1}S\sigma_{2}

where
\mathsf{lab}(R)=\mathsf{lab}(S)
.

Observe that the label of
R
does not appear anywhere along the sequence of steps
\rho_{1}
,

i.e.
that
\mathsf{lab}(R)\not\in\mathsf{labs}(\rho_{1})
, as a consequence of the
fact that no labels are ever repeated in any sequence of steps (Lem.
17
).
This implies that
\mathsf{lab}(S)\not\in\mathsf{labs}(\rho_{1}/\sigma_{1})
.
Indeed:

\mathsf{lab}(S)=\mathsf{lab}(R)\not\in\mathsf{labs}(\rho_{1})\supseteq\mathsf{labs}(\rho_{1})\setminus\mathsf{labs}(\sigma_{1})=^{(\text{Lem.~\ref{lemma:names_after_projection_along_a_step}})}\mathsf{labs}(\rho_{1}/\sigma_{1})

This means that
S
is not erased by the derivation
\rho_{1}/\sigma_{1}
.
More precisely,
S/(\rho_{1}/\sigma_{1})
is a singleton.

Symmetrically,
R/(\sigma_{1}/\rho_{1})
is a singleton.
Moreover,
\mathsf{lab}(S/(\rho_{1}/\sigma_{1}))=\mathsf{lab}(S)=\mathsf{lab}(R)=\mathsf{lab}(R/(\sigma_{1}/\rho_{1}))

so we have that
S/(\rho_{1}/\sigma_{1})=R/(\sigma_{1}/\rho_{1})
.
The situation is the following, where
\mathsf{labs}(\rho_{1})\cap\mathsf{labs}(\sigma_{1})=\varnothing
:

\scriptstyle{\rho_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{2}}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{1}/\rho_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{1}/\sigma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{S}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{2}}
\scriptstyle{R/(\sigma_{1}/\rho_{1})=S/(\rho_{1}/\sigma_{1})}

By Full stability (Lem.
15
) this means that there exists a step
T

such that
T/\rho_{1}=R
and
T/\sigma_{1}=S
.
Then
T\in\rho_{1}R\rho_{2}=\rho

and also
T\in\sigma_{1}S\sigma_{2}=\sigma

so
T
is common to
\rho
and
\sigma
,
by which we conclude.

Let
X
be the set of labels of the steps involved in some derivation to normal form.
More precisely, let
\rho_{0}:t\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}^{*}s
be a derivation to normal form, which exists
by virtue of
Strong Normalization
(Prop.
1
),
and let
X=\mathsf{labs}(\rho_{0})
.
Then we claim that:

\begin{array}[]{rrcl}\mathsf{labs}:&\mathbb{D}^{\texttt{{\#}}}(t)&\to&\mathcal{P}(X)\\
&{[\rho]}&\mapsto&\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\end{array}

is a monomorphism of lattices.
Note that
\mathsf{labs}
is well-defined
over permutation equivalence classes
since if
\rho\equiv\sigma
then

\mathsf{labs}(\rho)=\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)

by Coro.
4
.
We are to show that
\mathsf{labs}
is monotonic, that it preserves bottom, joins, top, and meets,
and finally that it is a monomorphism:

1.

Monotonic,
i.e.

{[\rho]}\sqsubseteq{[\sigma]}

implies
\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\subseteq\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)
.

By Prop.
12
.

2.

Preserves bottom.

Indeed,
\mathsf{labs}({[\epsilon]})=\varnothing
.

3.

Preserves joins,

i.e.

\mathsf{labs}({[\rho]}\sqcup{[\sigma]})=\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\cup\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)
.

Indeed:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\mathsf{labs}(\rho\sqcup\sigma)&=&\mathsf{labs}(\rho(\sigma/\rho))&\text{ by definition of $\sqcup$}\\
&=&\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\cup\mathsf{labs}(\sigma/\rho)\\
&=&\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\cup(\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)\setminus\mathsf{labs}(\rho))&\text{ by Lem.~\ref{lemma:names_after_projection_along_a_step}}\\
&=&\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\cup\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)\\
\end{array}

4.

Preserves top.

Recall that
\top={[\rho_{0}]}
where
\rho_{0}:t\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow_{\texttt{{\#}}}}^{*}s

is the derivation to normal form.
Note moreover that all derivations to normal form
are permutation equivalent in an orthogonal axiomatic rewrite system, so the choice
does not matter.
To conclude, observe that
\mathsf{labs}(\rho_{0})=X
by definition of
X
,
and
X
is indeed the top element of
\mathcal{P}(X)
.

5.

Preserves meets,

i.e.

\mathsf{labs}({[\rho]}\sqcap{[\sigma]})=\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\cap\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)
.

We show the two inclusions:

5.1

(\subseteq)

It suffices to check that

\mathsf{labs}(\rho\sqcap\sigma)\subseteq\mathsf{labs}(\rho)

(the inclusion

\mathsf{labs}(\rho\sqcap\sigma)\subseteq\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)

is symmetric).
By induction on the length of
\rho\sqcap\sigma
.
If
\rho\sqcap\sigma
is empty it is immediate.
If it is non-empty,

\rho\sqcap\sigma=T(\rho/T\sqcap\sigma/T)
,
where
T
is a step common to
\rho
and
\sigma
.
Then since
T
is common to
\rho
and
\sigma
,
we have that
\mathsf{lab}(T)\in\mathsf{labs}(\rho)
.
Moreover, by
i.h.

\mathsf{labs}(\rho/T\sqcap\sigma/T)\subseteq\mathsf{labs}(\rho/T)
.
So:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\mathsf{labs}(\rho\cap\sigma)&=&\{\mathsf{lab}(T)\}\cup\mathsf{labs}(\rho/T\sqcap\sigma/T)\\
&\subseteq&\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\cup\mathsf{labs}(\rho/T)&\text{by {i.h.} }\\
&=&\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\cup(\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\setminus\{\mathsf{lab}(T)\}&\text{by Lem.~\ref{lemma:names_after_projection_along_a_step}}\\
&=&\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\end{array}

5.2

(\supseteq)

To show
\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\cap\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)\subseteq\mathsf{labs}(\rho\sqcap\sigma)
,
we first prove the following claim:

Claim.

\mathsf{labs}(\rho/(\rho\sqcap\sigma))\cap\mathsf{labs}(\sigma/(\rho\sqcap\sigma)=\varnothing
.

Proof of the claim.

By Lem.
20
it suffices to show that

(\rho/(\rho\sqcap\sigma))\sqcap(\sigma/(\rho\sqcap\sigma))=\epsilon
.
By contradiction, suppose that there is a step
T
common to
the derivations

\rho/(\rho\sqcap\sigma)
and
\sigma/(\rho\sqcap\sigma)
.
Then the derivation
(\rho\sqcap\sigma)T
is a lower bound for
\{\rho,\sigma\}
,

i.e.

(\rho\sqcap\sigma)T\sqsubseteq\rho

and

(\rho\sqcap\sigma)T\sqsubseteq\sigma
.
Since
\rho\sqcap\sigma
is the greatest lower bound for
\{\rho,\sigma\}
,
we have that
(\rho\sqcap\sigma)T\sqsubseteq\rho\sqcap\sigma
.
But this implies that
T\sqsubseteq\epsilon
, which is a contradiction.

End of claim.

Note that
\rho\sqcap\sigma\sqsubseteq\rho
,
so we have that
\rho\equiv(\rho\sqcap\sigma)(\rho/(\rho\sqcap\sigma))
,
and this in turn implies that

\mathsf{labs}(\rho)=\mathsf{labs}((\rho\sqcap\sigma)(\rho/(\rho\sqcap\sigma)))

by Coro.
4
.
Symmetrically,

\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)=\mathsf{labs}((\rho\sqcap\sigma)(\sigma/(\rho\sqcap\sigma)))
.
Then:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}&&\mathsf{labs}(\rho)\cap\mathsf{labs}(\sigma)\\
&=&\mathsf{labs}((\rho\sqcap\sigma)(\rho/(\rho\sqcap\sigma)))\cap\mathsf{labs}((\rho\sqcap\sigma)(\sigma/(\rho\sqcap\sigma)))\\
&=&\left(\mathsf{labs}(\rho\sqcap\sigma)\cup\mathsf{labs}(\rho/(\rho\sqcap\sigma))\right)\cap\left(\mathsf{labs}(\rho\sqcap\sigma)\cup\mathsf{labs}(\sigma/(\rho\sqcap\sigma))\right)\\
&&\text{ by Rem.~\ref{remark:names_concatenation_union}}\\
&=&\mathsf{labs}(\rho\sqcap\sigma)\cup\left(\mathsf{labs}(\rho/(\rho\sqcap\sigma))\cap\mathsf{labs}(\sigma/(\rho\sqcap\sigma))\right)\\
&&\text{ since $(A\cup B)\cap(A\cup C)=A\cup(B\cap C)$ for arbitrary sets $A,B,C$}\\
&=&\mathsf{labs}(\rho\sqcap\sigma)\\
&&\text{ since $\left(\mathsf{labs}(\rho/(\rho\sqcap\sigma))\cap\mathsf{labs}(\sigma/(\rho\sqcap\sigma))\right)=\varnothing$ by the previous claim}\\
\end{array}

6.

Monomorphism.

It suffices to show that
\mathsf{labs}
is injective.
Indeed,
suppose that
\mathsf{labs}({[\rho]})=\mathsf{labs}({[\sigma]})
.
By Coro.
4

we have that
\rho\equiv\sigma
,
so

{[\rho]}={[\sigma]}
.

0.A.7
Proof of Prop.
6
– Simulation

To prove Prop.
6
we need an auxiliary lemma.

Lemma 21
(Refinement of a substitution: composition)

If
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
and
s^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\ltimes}s
for all
1\leq i\leq n
,
then
t^{\prime}\{x:=[s_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,s_{n}^{\prime}]\}\mathrel{\ltimes}t\{x:=s\}
.

Proof

Straightforward by induction on
t
.

We prove items 1 and 2 of Prop.
6
separately:

Item 1 of Prop.
6
: simulation of
\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}
with
\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}

Let
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
and let
t=\mathtt{C}\langle(\lambda x.p)q\rangle\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}\mathtt{C}\langle p\{x:=q\}\rangle=s
.
We prove that there exists a term
s^{\prime}
such that

t^{\prime}\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}^{*}s^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}s

by induction on
\mathtt{C}
.

1.

Empty context,
\mathtt{C}=\Box
.

Then
t=(\lambda x.p)q\to{p\{x:=q\}}=s

and
t^{\prime}
is of the form
(\lambda^{\ell}x.p^{\prime})[q_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,q_{n}^{\prime}]
.
We conclude by taking
s^{\prime}=p^{\prime}\{x:=[q_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,q_{n}^{\prime}]\}
, using Lem.
21
.

2.

Under an abstraction,
\mathtt{C}=\lambda y.\mathtt{C}^{\prime}
.

Immediate by
i.h.
.

3.

Left of an application,
\mathtt{C}=\mathtt{C}^{\prime}u
.

Immediate by
i.h.
.

4.

Right of an application,
\mathtt{C}=u\mathtt{C}^{\prime}
.

Then
t=u\,\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle(\lambda x.p)q\rangle\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}u\,\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle p\{x:=q\}\rangle=s

and
t^{\prime}=u^{\prime}[u^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,u^{\prime}_{n}]
,
with
u^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}u

and
u^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle(\lambda x.p)q\rangle

for all
i=1..n
.
Applying the
i.h.
on
u^{\prime}_{i}
for each
i=1..n
, we have:

\textstyle{\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle(\lambda x.p)q\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\rtimes}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle p\{x:=q\}\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\rtimes}
\textstyle{u^{\prime}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
#
\textstyle{u^{\prime\prime}_{i}}

So we may conclude as follows:

\textstyle{u\,\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle(\lambda x.p)q\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\rtimes}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{u\,\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle p\{x:=q\}\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\rtimes}
\textstyle{u^{\prime}[u^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,u^{\prime}_{n}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
#
\textstyle{u^{\prime}[u^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,u^{\prime\prime}_{n}]}

Item 2 of Prop.
6
: simulation of
\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}
with
\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}

Let
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
and
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}s^{\prime}
.
We prove that there exist terms
s
and
s^{\prime\prime}

such that

t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}s
,

s^{\prime}\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}^{*}s^{\prime\prime}
,
and
s^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}s

by induction on
t^{\prime}
.
Moreover,
s^{\prime}\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}^{*}s^{\prime\prime}
is a multistep.

1.

Variable
t^{\prime}=x
.
Impossible.

2.

Abstraction,
t^{\prime}=\lambda^{\ell}y.r^{\prime}

Immediate by
i.h.
.

3.

Application,
t^{\prime}=u^{\prime}[r^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,r^{\prime}_{n}]

Then
t=ur

where
u^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}u

and
r^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\ltimes}r
for all
i=1..n
.
There are three subcases,
depending on whether the step
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}s^{\prime}

takes place at the root,
inside
u^{\prime}
,
or inside
r^{\prime}_{i}
for some
i=1..n
,

3.1

Reduction at the root.

In this case
u^{\prime}=\lambda^{\ell}x.s^{\prime}
.
Then by Lem.
21
:

\textstyle{(\lambda^{\ell}x.s^{\prime})[r^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,r^{\prime}_{n}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
#
\textstyle{\mathrel{\ltimes}}
\textstyle{(\lambda x.s)r\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{s^{\prime}\{x:=[r^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,r^{\prime}_{n}]\}}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\ltimes}}
\textstyle{s\{x:=r\}}

3.2

Reduction in
u^{\prime}
.

Immediate by
i.h.
.

3.3

Reduction in
r^{\prime}_{i}
for some
i=1..n
.

We have that
r^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}r^{\prime\prime}_{i}
, so by
i.h.
there exist
p^{\prime}_{i}
and
p
such that:

\textstyle{r^{\prime}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
#
\textstyle{\mathrel{\ltimes}}
\textstyle{r\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{r^{\prime\prime}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
#
\textstyle{p^{\prime}_{i}}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\ltimes}}
\textstyle{p}

For every
j=1..n
,
j\neq i
,
we have that
r^{\prime}_{j}\mathrel{\ltimes}r
and

r\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}p
.
By item 1 of Simulation (Prop.
6
),
which we have just proved, we have the following diagram:

\textstyle{r^{\prime}_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
#
\textstyle{\mathrel{\ltimes}}
\textstyle{r\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{p^{\prime}_{j}}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\ltimes}}
\textstyle{p}

So:

\textstyle{u^{\prime}[r^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,r^{\prime}_{j-1},r^{\prime}_{j},r^{\prime}_{j+1},\ldots,r^{\prime}_{n}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
#
\textstyle{\mathrel{\ltimes}}
\textstyle{u\,r\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{u^{\prime}[r^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,r^{\prime}_{j-1},r^{\prime\prime}_{j},r^{\prime}_{j+1},\ldots,r^{\prime}_{n}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
#
\textstyle{u^{\prime}[p^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,p^{\prime}_{j-1},p^{\prime}_{j},p^{\prime}_{j+1},\ldots,p^{\prime}_{n}]}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\ltimes}}
\textstyle{u\,p}

0.A.8
Proof of Prop.
7
– Typability characterizes head normalization

Before proving Prop.
7
, we need a few auxiliary results.

Definition 16
(Head normal forms)

A
\lambda
-term
t
is a
head normal form
if it is of the form

t=\lambda x_{1}.\ldots\lambda x_{n}.y\,t_{1}\,\ldots\,t_{m}

where
n\geq 0
and
m\geq 0
.
Note that
y
might be or not be among the variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
.
Similarly, a correct term
t\in\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
of
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
a head normal form
if it is of the form

t=\lambda^{\ell_{1}}x_{1}.\ldots\lambda^{\ell_{n}}x_{n}.y^{\mathcal{A}}\,\bar{t}_{1}\,\ldots\,\bar{t}_{m}

where
n\geq 0
and
m\geq 0
.

Lemma 22

If
t
is a head normal form,
there exists
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
such that
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
.
Moreover
t^{\prime}
can be taken to be of the form

\lambda^{\ell_{1}}x_{1}.\ldots\lambda^{\ell_{n}}x_{n}.y^{[\,]\overset{\ell^{\prime}_{1}}{\to}\ldots[\,]\overset{\ell^{\prime}_{m}}{\to}\alpha^{\ell^{\prime\prime}}}[\,]\ldots[\,]
.

Proof

Let
t=\lambda x_{1}.\ldots\lambda x_{n}.yt_{1}\ldots t_{m}
.
Let
\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n},\ell^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\ell^{\prime}_{m},\ell^{\prime\prime}\}

be a set of
n+m+1
pairwise distinct labels and let
\alpha
be a base type.
Take
t^{\prime}:=\lambda^{\ell_{1}}x_{1}.\ldots\lambda^{\ell_{n}}x_{n}.y^{[\,]\overset{\ell^{\prime}_{1}}{\to}\ldots[\,]\overset{\ell^{\prime}_{m}}{\to}\alpha^{\ell^{\prime\prime}}}\underbrace{[\,]\ldots[\,]}_{\text{$m$ times}}
.
Observe that
t^{\prime}
is correct and
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
.

Lemma 23
(
\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}
-normal forms refine head normal forms)

Let
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
be a
\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}
-normal form
and
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
. Then
t
is a head normal form.

Proof

Observe, by induction on
t^{\prime}

that if
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
is a
\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}
-normal form, it must be
of the form

\lambda^{\ell_{1}}x_{1}.\ldots\lambda^{\ell_{n}}x_{n}.y^{\mathcal{A}}\bar{s}_{1}\ldots\bar{s}_{m}
,
as it cannot have a subterm of the form
(\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}z.u)\bar{r}
.
Then
\lambda^{\ell_{1}}x_{1}.\ldots\lambda^{\ell_{n}}x_{n}.y^{\mathcal{A}}\bar{s}_{1}\ldots\bar{s}_{m}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
.
So
t
is of the form
\lambda x_{1}.\ldots\lambda x_{n}.ys_{1}\ldots s_{n}
,
that is,
t
is a head normal form.

The following lemma is an adaptation of Subject Expansion in
[
8
]
.

Lemma 24
(Subject Expansion)

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathtt{C}\langle t\{x:=\bar{s}\}\rangle:\mathcal{A}
is derivable,
then
\Gamma\vdash\mathtt{C}\langle(\lambda^{\ell}x.t)\bar{s}\rangle:\mathcal{A}
is derivable.

Proof

The proof proceeds by induction on
\mathtt{C}
,
and the base case by induction on
t
,
similar to the proof that substitution preserves typing in
the proof of Subject Reduction (Sec.
0.A.1
).

Correctness is not necessarily preserved by
\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}
-expansion.
We need a stronger invariant,
strong sequentiality
,
that will be shown to be preserved by expansion
under appropiate conditions:

Definition 17
(Subterms and free subterms)

The set of
subterms

\mathsf{sub}(t)
of a term
t
is formally defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathsf{sub}(x^{\mathcal{A}})&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\{x^{\mathcal{A}}\}\\
\mathsf{sub}(\lambda^{\ell}x.t)&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\{\lambda^{\ell}x.t\}\cup\mathsf{sub}(t)\\
\mathsf{sub}(t[s_{i}]_{i=1}^{n})&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\{t[s_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}\}\cup\mathsf{sub}(t)\cup\cup_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{sub}(s_{i})\end{array}

The set of
free subterms

\mathsf{sub}^{\circ}(t)
of a term
t
is defined similarly,
except for the abstraction case, which requires that the subterm in question
do not include occurrences of bound variables:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathsf{sub}^{\circ}(x^{\mathcal{A}})&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\{x^{\mathcal{A}}\}\\
\mathsf{sub}^{\circ}(\lambda^{\ell}x.t)&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\{\lambda^{\ell}x.t\}\cup\{u\in\mathsf{sub}^{\circ}(t)\ |\ x\not\in\mathsf{fv}({u})\}\\
\mathsf{sub}^{\circ}(t[s_{i}]_{i=1}^{n})&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\{t[s_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}\}\cup\mathsf{sub}^{\circ}(t)\cup\cup_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{sub}^{\circ}(s_{i})\end{array}

Definition 18
(Strong sequentiality)

A term
t
is
strongly sequential

if it is correct and, moreover,
for every subterm
s\in\mathsf{sub}(t)

and any two free subterms
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathsf{sub}^{\circ}(s)
lying at disjoint positions of
s
,
the types of
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
have different external labels.

Example 6

The following examples illustrate the notion of strong sequentiality:

1.

The term
t=(\lambda^{1}x.y^{\alpha^{2}})[\,]
is strongly sequential.
Note that
t
and
y^{\alpha^{2}}
have the same type, namely
\alpha^{2}
,
but they do not occur at
disjoint
positions.

2.

The term
t=(\lambda^{1}x.x^{\alpha^{2}})[y^{\alpha^{2}}]
is strongly sequential.
Note that
x^{\alpha^{2}}
and
y^{\alpha^{2}}
both have type
\alpha^{2}
,
but they are not simultaneously free subterms of the same subterm of
t
.

3.

The term
t=\lambda^{1}y.x^{[\alpha^{2}]\overset{3}{\to}[\alpha^{2}]\overset{4}{\to}\beta^{5}}[y^{\alpha^{2}}][z^{\alpha^{2}}]

is not strongly sequential,
since
y^{\alpha^{2}}
and
z^{\alpha^{2}}

have the same type and they are both free subterms of

x^{[\alpha^{2}]\overset{3}{\to}[\alpha^{2}]\overset{4}{\to}\beta^{5}}[y^{\alpha^{2}}][z^{\alpha^{2}}]\in\mathsf{sub}(t)
.

Lemma 25
(Refinement of a substitution: decomposition)

If
u^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t\{x:=s\}

and
u^{\prime}
is strongly sequential,
then
u^{\prime}
is of the form
t^{\prime}\{x:=[s^{\prime}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}\}
.
Moreover, given a fresh label
\ell
,
the term
(\lambda^{\ell}x.t^{\prime})[s^{\prime}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}
is strongly sequential,

t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
and
s^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\ltimes}s
for all
i=1..n
.

Proof

By induction on
t
.

1.

Variable (same),
t=x
.

Then
u^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}s
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be the type of
u^{\prime}
.
Taking
t^{\prime}:=x^{\mathcal{A}}\mathrel{\ltimes}x

we have that
(\lambda^{\ell}x.x^{\mathcal{A}})[u^{\prime}]
is strongly sequential.
Regarding strong sequentiality,
observe that
x^{\mathcal{A}}
and
u^{\prime}
have the same type, but they are not
simultaneously the free subterms of any subterm of
(\lambda^{\ell}x.x^{\mathcal{A}})[u^{\prime}]
.

2.

Variable (different),
t=y\neq x
.

Then
u^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}y
, so
u^{\prime}
is of the form
y^{\mathcal{A}}
.
Taking
t^{\prime}:=y^{\mathcal{A}}
we have that

(\lambda^{\ell}x.y^{\mathcal{A}})[\,]
is strongly sequential.

3.

Abstraction,
t=\lambda y.r
.

Then
u^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\lambda y.r\{x:=s\}

so
u^{\prime}
is of the form
\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.u^{\prime\prime}

where
u^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}r\{x:=s\}
.
By
i.h.
,
u^{\prime\prime}
is of the form
r^{\prime}\{x:=[s^{\prime}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}\}

where
(\lambda^{\ell}x.r^{\prime})[s^{\prime}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}
is strongly sequential,

r^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}r
and
s^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\ltimes}s
for all
i=1..n
.
Taking
t^{\prime}:=\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.r^{\prime}
,
we have that

t^{\prime}=\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.r^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\lambda y.r=t
.
Moreover, the term

(\lambda^{\ell}x.t^{\prime})[s_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}=(\lambda^{\ell}x.\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.r^{\prime})[s_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}

is strongly sequential.
Typability is a consequence of Subject Expansion (Lem.
24
).
The remaining properties are:

3.1

Uniquely labeled lambdas.

The multiset of labels decorating the lambdas of

(\lambda^{\ell}x.\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.r^{\prime})[s_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}

is given by

\Lambda((\lambda^{\ell}x.\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.r^{\prime})[s_{i}]_{i=1}^{n})=[\ell,\ell^{\prime}]+\Lambda(r^{\prime})+_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda(s_{i})
.
It suffices to check that this multiset has no repeats.
The label
\ell
is assumed to be fresh, so it occurs only once.
By
i.h.
,
u^{\prime\prime}=r^{\prime}\{x:=[s^{\prime}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}\}
,
so using Lem.
9
we have

\Lambda(u^{\prime})=\Lambda(\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.u^{\prime})=[\ell^{\prime}]+\Lambda(u^{\prime\prime})=[\ell^{\prime}]+\Lambda(r^{\prime}\{x:=[s^{\prime}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}\})=[\ell^{\prime}]+\Lambda(r^{\prime})+_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda(s^{\prime}_{i})
.
Moreover, the term
u^{\prime}=\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.u^{\prime\prime}
is correct,
so this multiset has no repeats.

3.2

Sequential contexts.

Let
q
be a subterm of
(\lambda^{\ell}x.\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.r^{\prime})[s^{\prime}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}
.
If
q
is a subterm of
r^{\prime}
or a subterm of
s^{\prime}_{i}
for some
i=1..n
we conclude by
i.h.
since
(\lambda^{\ell}x.r^{\prime})[s^{\prime}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}
is known to be correct.
Moreover, if
\Gamma\oplus x:\mathcal{M}\oplus y:\mathcal{N}
is the typing context for
r^{\prime}
,
the typing contexts of

\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.r^{\prime}
and
\lambda^{\ell}x.\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.r^{\prime}

are respectively
\Gamma\oplus x:\mathcal{M}
and
\Gamma
, which are also sequential.
Finally, if
\Delta_{i}
is the typing context for
s^{\prime}_{i}
, for each
i=1..n
,
the typing context for
(\lambda^{\ell}x.r^{\prime})[s^{\prime}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}

is of the form
\Gamma+_{i=1}^{n}\Delta_{i}+y:\mathcal{N}
, and it is sequential by
i.h.
.
Hence the typing context for the whole term is
\Gamma+_{i=1}^{n}\Delta_{i}
, and it is sequential.

3.3

Sequential types.

Let
q
be a subterm
(\lambda^{\ell}x.\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.r^{\prime})[s^{\prime}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}
,
and let
\mathcal{P}\overset{\ell^{\prime\prime}}{\to}\mathcal{C}
be a type that occurs in the typing context
or the type of
q
.
As in the previous case, we have that

\Gamma\oplus x:[\mathcal{B}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}\oplus y:\mathcal{N}\vdash r^{\prime}:\mathcal{A}
is derivable
and
\Delta_{i}\vdash s^{\prime}_{i}:\mathcal{B}_{i}
is derivable for all
i=1..n
.
Moreover, they are correct by
i.h.
, so if
q
is a subterm of
r^{\prime}
or a subterm of some
s^{\prime}_{i}
,
we are done.
There are three cases left for
q
:

3.3.1

Case
q=\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.r^{\prime}
.

The typing context is
\Gamma\oplus x:[\mathcal{B}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}
and the type
\mathcal{N}\overset{\ell^{\prime}}{\to}\mathcal{A}
.

3.3.2

Case
q=\lambda^{\ell}x.\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.r^{\prime}
.

The typing context is
\Gamma
and the type
[\mathcal{B}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}\overset{\ell}{\to}\mathcal{N}\overset{\ell^{\prime}}{\to}\mathcal{A}
.

3.3.3

Case
q=(\lambda^{\ell}x.\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.r^{\prime})[s_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}
.

The typing context is
\Gamma
and the type
\mathcal{N}\overset{\ell^{\prime}}{\to}\mathcal{A}
.

In all three cases, if
\mathcal{P}\overset{\ell^{\prime\prime}}{\to}\mathcal{C}
occurs in the typing context
or the type of
q
, then
\mathcal{P}
can be shown to be sequential using the
i.h.
.

3.4

Strong sequentiality.

Let
q\in\mathsf{sub}((\lambda^{\ell}x.\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.r^{\prime})[s_{i}]_{i=1}^{n})

be a subterm,
and let
q_{1},q_{2}\in\mathsf{sub}^{\circ}(q)
be free subterms lying at disjoint positions of
q
.
We argue that the types of
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
have different external labels.
Consider the following five possibilities for
q_{1}
:

3.4.1

Case
q_{1}=(\lambda^{\ell}x.\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.r^{\prime})[s_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}
.

Impossible since
q_{2}
must be at a disjoint position.

3.4.2

Case
q_{1}=\lambda^{\ell}x.\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.r^{\prime}
.

Then the external label of the type of
q_{1}
is the label
\ell
, which is fresh,
so it cannot coincide with the type of any other subterm.

3.4.3

Case
q_{1}=\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.r^{\prime}
.

Then
q_{2}
must be a subterm of
s_{i}
for some
i=1..n
.
Note that
q
must be the whole term,
and
n>0
, so there is at least one free occurrence of
x
in
\lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}y.r^{\prime}
.
This means that
q_{1}\not\in\mathsf{sub}^{\circ}(q)
, so this case is impossible.

3.4.4

Case
q_{1}
is a subterm of
r^{\prime}
.

If
q_{2}
is also a subterm of
r^{\prime}
, we conclude since by
i.h.

(\lambda^{\ell}x.r^{\prime})[s^{\prime}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}
is strongly sequential.
Otherwise,
q_{2}
is a subterm of
s_{i}
for some
i=1..n
,
and we also conclude by
i.h.
.

3.4.5

Case
q_{1}
is a subterm of
s_{i}
for some
i=1..n
.

If
q_{2}
is a subterm of
s_{j}
for some
j=1..n
,
we conclude since by
i.h.

(\lambda^{\ell}x.r^{\prime})[s^{\prime}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}
is strongly sequential.
If
q_{2}
is any other subterm, note that the symmetric case has already been considered
in one of the previous cases.

4.

Application,
t=rp
.

Then
u^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}(rp)\{x:=s\}
,
so it is of the form
u^{\prime}_{0}[u^{\prime}_{j}]_{j=1}^{m}

where
u^{\prime}_{0}\mathrel{\ltimes}r\{x:=s\}

and
u^{\prime}_{j}\mathrel{\ltimes}p\{x:=s\}
for all
j=1..m
.
By
i.h.
we have that
u^{\prime}_{0}
is of the form

r^{\prime}\{x:=\bar{s}_{0}\}

and for all
j=1..m
the term
u^{\prime}_{j}
is of the form

p^{\prime}_{j}\{x:=\bar{s}_{j}\}
,
where
r^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}r

and
p^{\prime}_{j}\mathrel{\ltimes}p
for all
j=1..m
.
Moreover,
the length of the list
\bar{s}_{j}
is
k_{j}

and

\bar{s}_{j}=[s^{(j)}_{i}]_{i=1}^{k_{j}}
for all
j=0..m
,
and we have that
s^{(j)}_{i}\mathrel{\ltimes}s
for all
j=0..m,i=1..k_{j}
.
By
i.h.
we also know that

(\lambda^{\ell}x.r^{\prime})\bar{s}_{0}
is strongly sequential
and
(\lambda^{\ell}x.p^{\prime}_{j})\bar{s}_{j}
is strongly sequential for all
j=1..m
.

Let
t^{\prime}:=r^{\prime}[p^{\prime}_{j}]_{j=1}^{m}
,
let
n:=\sum_{j=0}^{m}k_{j}
,
and let
[s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}]:=\sum_{j=0}^{m}\bar{s}_{j}
.
Note that
t^{\prime}=r^{\prime}[p^{\prime}_{j}]_{j=1}^{m}\mathrel{\ltimes}rp=t

and
s^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\ltimes}s
for all
i=1..n
.

Moreover, we have to check that
u^{\prime}=t^{\prime}\{x:=[s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}]\}
.
To prove this,
note that
t^{\prime}\{x:=[s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}]\}=(r^{\prime}[p^{\prime}_{j}]_{j=1}^{m})\{x:=[s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}]\}
.
Suppose that the multiset
\mathtt{T}([s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}])
were sequential.
Then the list of terms
[s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}]
would be partitioned
as
(\bar{u}_{0},\ldots,\bar{u}_{m})

where
\bar{u}_{j}
is a permutation of
\bar{s}_{j}
for all
j=0..m
,
and we would have indeed:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}t^{\prime}\{x:=[s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}]\}&=&(r^{\prime}[p^{\prime}_{j}]_{j=1}^{m})\{x:=[s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}]\}\\
&=&r^{\prime}\{x:=\bar{u}_{0}\}[p^{\prime}_{j}\{x:=\bar{u}_{j}\}]_{j=1}^{m})\hfill\text{ by $\mathtt{T}([s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}])$ sequential}\\
&=&r^{\prime}\{x:=\bar{s}_{0}\}[p^{\prime}_{j}\{x:=\bar{s}_{j}\}]_{j=1}^{m})\\
&=&r^{\prime}\{x:=\bar{s}_{0}\}[p^{\prime}_{j}\{x:=\bar{s}_{j}\}]_{j=1}^{m})\hfill\text{by Lem.~\ref{lemma:substitution_permutation}}\\
&=&u^{\prime}_{0}[u^{\prime}_{j}]_{j=1}^{m}=u^{\prime}\hfill\text{by {i.h.} }\end{array}

To see that
\mathtt{T}([s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}])
is sequential,
note that for every
i\neq j
,
the terms
s^{\prime}_{i}
and
s^{\prime}_{j}
are free subterms of
u^{\prime}

and they lie at disjoint positions of
u^{\prime}
.
Since
u^{\prime}
is strongly sequential, the types of
s^{\prime}_{i}
and
s^{\prime}_{j}

have different external labels. Hence
\mathtt{T}([s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}])
is sequential.

To conclude, we are left to check that
(\lambda^{\ell}x.t^{\prime})[s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}]
is strongly sequential:

4.1

Uniquely labeled lambdas.

The multiset of labels decorating the lambdas of
(\lambda^{\ell}x.t^{\prime})[s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}]

is given by

\Lambda((\lambda^{\ell}x.t^{\prime})[s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}])=[\ell]+\Lambda(t^{\prime})+_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda(s^{\prime}_{i})
.
It suffices to check that this multiset has no repeats.
The label
\ell
is assumed to be fresh, so it occurs only once.
We have already argued that

u^{\prime}=t^{\prime}\{x:=[s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}]\}
,
so using Lem.
9
we have

\Lambda(u^{\prime})=\Lambda(t^{\prime})+_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda(s^{\prime}_{i})
.
Moreover
u^{\prime}
is correct, so this multiset has no repeats.

4.2

Sequential contexts.

Suppose that

\Gamma_{0}\oplus x:\mathcal{M}_{0}\vdash r^{\prime}:[\mathcal{C}_{j}]_{j=1}^{m}\overset{\ell^{\prime}}{\to}\mathcal{A}

is derivable,

\Gamma_{j}\oplus x:\mathcal{M}_{j}\vdash p^{\prime}_{j}:\mathcal{C}_{j}

is derivable for all
j=1..m
,
and
\Delta_{i}\vdash s^{\prime}_{i}:\mathcal{B}_{i}
is derivable for all
i=1..n
.
Note that
\sum_{j=0}^{m}\mathcal{M}_{j}=[\mathcal{B}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}
.

Let
q
be a subterm of

(\lambda^{\ell}x.r^{\prime}[p^{\prime}_{j}]_{j=1}^{m})[s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}]
.
Consider four cases for
q
:

4.2.1

Case
q=(\lambda^{\ell}x.r^{\prime}[p^{\prime}_{j}]_{j=1}^{m})[s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}]
.

The typing context is
\sum_{j=0}^{m}\Gamma_{j}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Delta_{i}
.
By Subject Expansion (Lem.
24
)
the typing context of
(r^{\prime}[p^{\prime}_{j}]_{j=1}^{m})\{x:=[s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}]\}=u^{\prime}

is also
\sum_{j=0}^{m}\Gamma_{j}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Delta_{i}

and
u^{\prime}
is correct by hypothesis.
So
\sum_{j=0}^{m}\Gamma_{j}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Delta_{i}
is sequential.

4.2.2

Case
q=\lambda^{\ell}x.r^{\prime}[p^{\prime}_{j}]_{j=1}^{m}
.

The typing context is

\sum_{j=0}^{m}\Gamma_{j}
,
which is sequential because
\sum_{j=0}^{m}\Gamma_{j}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Delta_{i}
is sequential.

4.2.3

Case
q=r^{\prime}[p^{\prime}_{j}]_{j=1}^{m}
.

The typing context is

\sum_{j=0}^{m}\Gamma_{j}\oplus x:[\mathcal{B}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}
,
which is sequential because
\sum_{j=0}^{m}\Gamma_{j}
is sequential
and, moreover,
[\mathcal{B}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}=\mathtt{T}([\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{n}])

which we have already shown to be sequential.

4.2.4

Otherwise.

Then
q
is a subterm of
r^{\prime}
, a subterm of
p^{\prime}_{j}
for some
j=1..m
,
or a subterm of some
s^{\prime}_{i}
for some
i=1..n
.
Then we conclude since by
i.h.

(\lambda^{\ell}x.r^{\prime})\bar{s}_{0}

and all the
(\lambda^{\ell}x.p^{\prime}_{j})\bar{s}_{j}

are strongly sequential.

4.3

Sequential types.

Let
q
be a subterm of
(\lambda^{\ell}x.r^{\prime}[p^{\prime}_{j}]_{j=1}^{m})[s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}]
.
We claim that if
\mathcal{N}\overset{\ell^{\prime\prime}}{\to}\mathcal{D}
occurs in the context or in the type of
q
,
then
\mathcal{N}
is sequential.
The proof is similar as for subcase .

4.4

Strong sequentiality.

Let
q\in\mathsf{sub}((\lambda^{\ell}x.r^{\prime}[p^{\prime}_{j}]_{j=1}^{m})[s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}])

be a subterm,
and let
q_{1},q_{2}\in\mathsf{sub}^{\circ}(q)
be free subterms lying at disjoint positions of
q
.
We claim that the types of
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
have different external labels.
The proof is similar as for subcase .

Lemma 26
(Backwards Simulation)

Let
t,s\in\mathcal{T}
be
\lambda
-terms and
let
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
be a strongly sequential term such that

t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}s
and
s^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}s
.
Then there exists a strongly sequential term
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
such that:

\textstyle{t\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\rtimes}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{s\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\rtimes}
\textstyle{t^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
#
\textstyle{s^{\prime}}

Proof

Let
t=\mathtt{C}\langle(\lambda x.u)r\rangle\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}\mathtt{C}\langle u\{x:=r\}\rangle=s
.
The proof proceeds by induction on
\mathtt{C}
.

1.

Empty,
\mathtt{C}=\Box
.

By Lem.
25
we have that
s^{\prime}

is of the form
u^{\prime}\{x:=[r^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,r^{\prime}_{n}]\}

where
u^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}u
and
r^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\ltimes}r
for all
i=1..n
.
Moreover, taking
\ell
to be a fresh label,
(\lambda^{\ell}x.u^{\prime})[r^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,r^{\prime}_{n}]

is strongly sequential and

(\lambda^{\ell}x.u^{\prime})[r^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,r^{\prime}_{n}]\mathrel{\ltimes}(\lambda x.u)r
.

2.

Under an abstraction,
\mathtt{C}=\lambda x.\mathtt{C}^{\prime}
.

Straightforward by
i.h.
.

3.

Left of an application,
\mathtt{C}=\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\,u

Straightforward by
i.h.
.

4.

Right of an application,
\mathtt{C}=u\,\mathtt{C}^{\prime}

Then
t=u\,r\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}u\,p=s

where
r\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}p
and
s^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}s
.
Then
s^{\prime}
is of the form
u^{\prime}[p^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,p^{\prime}_{n}]
where
p^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\ltimes}p
for all
i=1..n
.
By
i.h.
, for all
i=1..n
we have that there exist
r^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,r^{\prime}_{n}
such that:

\textstyle{r\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\rtimes}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{p\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\rtimes}
\textstyle{r^{\prime}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
#
\textstyle{p^{\prime}_{i}}

So we have:
\textstyle{u\,r\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\rtimes}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{up\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\rtimes}
\textstyle{u^{\prime}[r^{\prime}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
#
\textstyle{u^{\prime}[p^{\prime}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}}

Moreover,
u^{\prime}[r^{\prime}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}
is strongly sequential, which can be concluded
from the facts that
u^{\prime}[p^{\prime}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}
is strongly sequential by hypothesis,

r^{\prime}_{i}
is strongly sequential for all
i=1..n
by
i.h.
,
and
r^{\prime}_{i}
and
p^{\prime}_{i}
have the same types by Subject Expansion (Lem.
24
).

To prove the equivalence
(1\iff 2)
of Prop.
7
, we claim that the following
three statements are equivalent:

1.

There exists
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
such that
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
.

1’.

There exists
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
such that
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t

and
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}^{*}\lambda^{\ell_{1}}x_{1}.\ldots\lambda^{\ell_{n}}x_{n}.y^{\mathcal{A}}[]\ldots[]
.

2.

There exists a head normal form
s
such that
t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}^{*}s
.

Let us prove the chain of implications
1\implies 2\implies 1^{\prime}\implies 1
:

•

(1\implies 2)

Let
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
.
By Strong Normalization (Prop.
1
), reduce
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}^{*}s^{\prime}

to normal form.
We claim that there exists a term
s
such that
t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}^{*}s
and
s^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}s
.
Observe that, since
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
is strongly normalizing (Prop.
1
)
and finitely branching, König’s lemma ensures that there is a bound for the length of
\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}
-derivations
going out from a term
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
.
(Alternatively, according to the proof of Strong Normalization in Prop.
1
,
the bound may be explicitly taken to be the number of lambdas in
t^{\prime}
).
Call this bound the
weight
of
t^{\prime}
.

We proceed by induction on the weight of
t^{\prime}
.
If the derivation is empty, we are done by taking
s:=t
.
If the derivation is non-empty, it is of the form
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}u^{\prime}\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}^{*}s^{\prime}
.
By Simulation (Prop.
6
) there exist terms
u
and
u^{\prime\prime}

such that
u^{\prime}\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}^{*}u^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}u

and
t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}u
. Since the
\lambda^{\texttt{{\#}}}
-calculus is
confluent (Prop.
2
) and
s^{\prime}
is a normal form,
we have that
u^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow_{\texttt{{\#}}}}s^{\prime}
.
Note that the weight of
t^{\prime}
is strictly larger than the weight of
u^{\prime\prime}
,
so by
i.h.
there exists
s
such that
u\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}^{*}s

and
s^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}s
:

\textstyle{t\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\rtimes}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{u\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\rtimes}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{s\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\rtimes}
\textstyle{t^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
#
\textstyle{u^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
#
#
\textstyle{u^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
#
\textstyle{s^{\prime}}

Finally, since
s^{\prime}
is a
\mathrel{\to_{\texttt{{\#}}}}
-normal form and
s^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}s
,
Lem.
23

ensures that
s
is a head normal form, as required.

•

(1^{\prime}\implies 1)
Obvious.

•

(2\implies 1^{\prime})

Let
t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}^{*}s
be a derivation to head normal form.
We claim that there exists
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
such that
t^{\prime}
is strongly sequential,
and the normal form of
t^{\prime}
is of the form
\lambda^{\ell_{1}}x_{1}.\ldots\lambda^{\ell_{n}}x_{n}.y^{\mathcal{A}}[]\ldots[]
.
By induction on the length of the derivation
t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}^{*}s
.
If the derivation is empty,
t=s
is a head normal form
and we conclude by Lem.
22
, observing that the constructed term
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t

is strongly sequential.
If the derivation is non-empty, conclude using the
i.h.
and Backwards Simulation (Lem.
26
).

0.A.9
Proof of Prop.
8
– Compatibility

We need a few auxiliary results.

Lemma 27
(Simulation residuals and composition)

If
\rho,\sigma
are composable derivations and
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathsf{src}(\rho)
,
then:

1.

t^{\prime}/\rho\sigma=(t^{\prime}/\rho)/\sigma

2.

\rho\sigma/t^{\prime}=(\rho/t^{\prime})(\sigma/(t^{\prime}/\rho))

Proof

Straightforward by induction on
\rho
.

An
n
-hole context
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}
is a term with
n
ocurrences of a hole
\Box
.
We extend refinement for contexts by setting
\Box\mathrel{\ltimes}\Box
.
We write
\mathtt{C}\langle t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\rangle
for the capturing substitution of the

i
-th occurrence of
\Box
in
\mathtt{C}
by
t_{i}
for all
i=1..n
.
If
R:t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}s
is a step, we write
\mathtt{C}\langle R\rangle

for the step
\mathtt{C}\langle R\rangle:\mathtt{C}\langle t\rangle\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}\mathtt{C}\langle s\rangle
.
Note that in general a context
\mathtt{C}
may be refined by an
n
-hole context,
for example a
0
-hole context,
x^{[]\overset{1}{\to}\alpha^{2}}[\,]\mathrel{\ltimes}x(y\,\Box)
,
or a
2
-hole context,
x^{[\alpha^{1},\beta^{2}]\overset{3}{\to}\gamma^{4}}[\Box,\Box]\mathrel{\ltimes}x\Box
.

Lemma 28
(Simulation of contexts)

Let
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}
,
let
t^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,t^{\prime}_{n}\in\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
and
t\in\mathcal{T}

such that
t^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\ltimes}t^{\prime}
for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
and
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle t^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,t^{\prime}_{n}\rangle\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}\langle t\rangle
.
Moreover, let
R:t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}s
.
Then
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle t^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,t^{\prime}_{n}\rangle/\mathtt{C}\langle R\rangle=\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle t_{1}/R,\ldots,t_{n}/R\rangle
,
and
\mathsf{labs}(\mathtt{C}\langle R\rangle/\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle t^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,t^{\prime}_{n}\rangle)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{labs}(R/t^{\prime}_{i})
.

Proof

Straightforward by induction on
\mathtt{C}
.

In the proof of the following lemma (Lem.
29
),
sometimes we will use the previous lemma (Lem.
28
) implicitly.

Lemma 29
(Basic Cube Lemma for simulation residuals)

Let
R:t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}s
and
S:t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}u
be coinitial steps,
and let
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
be a correct term such that
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
.
Then the following equality between sets of coinitial steps holds:

(R/t^{\prime})/(S/t^{\prime})=(R/S)/(t^{\prime}/S)
.

Proof

If
R=S
then it is easy to see that the proposition holds, so we can assume that
R\neq S
.
Also, note that it is enough to see that
\mathsf{labs}((R/t^{\prime})/(S/t^{\prime}))=\mathsf{labs}(((R/S)/(t^{\prime}/S))
, as we will do that in some cases.
We proceed by induction on
t
.

1.

Variable
t=x
.
Impossible.

2.

Abstraction,
t=\lambda x.u
.

Immediate by
i.h.
.

3.

Application,
t=pq
.

Three cases, depending on the position of
R
.

3.1

If
R
is at the root.

Then
t=(\lambda x.r)q
, and
R:(\lambda x.r)q\to r\{x:=q\}
.
Then
S
may be inside
r
or inside
q
. In any case,
the situation is:

\textstyle{(\lambda x.r)q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{S}
\textstyle{r\{x:=q\}}
\textstyle{(\lambda x.r^{\circ})q^{\circ}}
\textstyle{(\lambda^{\ell}x.r^{\prime})\bar{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R/t^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{S/t^{\prime}}
\textstyle{r^{\prime}\{x:=\bar{q}\}}
\textstyle{(\lambda^{\ell}x.r^{\prime\circ})\bar{q}^{\circ}}

Note that
(R/t^{\prime})/(S/t^{\prime})
has only one element
R_{1}
,
namely the step contracting the lambda labeled with
\ell
.
Note that
R/S
also happens to have only one element,

R_{2}:(\lambda x.r^{\circ})q^{\circ}\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}r^{\circ}\{x:=q^{\circ}\}
.
It is easy to see that
R_{2}/((\lambda^{\ell}x.r^{\prime})\bar{q})=\{R_{1}\}
, as required.

3.2

If
R
is in
p
.

We consider three subcases, depending on the position of
S
.

3.2.1

If
S
is at the root.

Then
p=\lambda x.\mathtt{C}\langle(\lambda y.u)v\rangle
and
(for appropiate
\mathtt{C}^{\circ},u^{\circ},v^{\circ}
):

\textstyle{(\lambda x.\mathtt{C}\langle(\lambda y.u)v\rangle)q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{S}
\textstyle{(\lambda x.\mathtt{C}\langle u\{y:=v\}\rangle)q}
\textstyle{\mathtt{C}^{\circ}\langle(\lambda y.u^{\circ})v^{\circ}\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R/S}
\textstyle{\mathtt{C}^{\circ}\langle u^{\circ}\{y:=v^{\circ}\}\rangle}

\textstyle{(\lambda^{\ell}x.\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle(\lambda^{\ell_{1}}y.u_{1})v_{1},\ldots,(\lambda^{\ell_{n}}y.u_{n})v_{n}\rangle)\bar{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R/t^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{S/t^{\prime}}
\textstyle{(\lambda^{\ell}x.\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle u_{1}\{y:=v_{1}\},\ldots,u_{n}\{y:=v_{n}\}\rangle)\bar{q}}
\textstyle{\mathtt{C}^{\prime\circ}\langle(\lambda^{\ell_{1}}y.u^{\circ}_{1})v^{\circ}_{1},\ldots,(\lambda^{\ell_{n}}y.u^{\circ}_{n})v^{\circ}_{n}\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(R/t^{\prime})/(S/t^{\prime})}
\textstyle{\mathtt{C}^{\prime\circ}\langle u^{\circ}_{1}\{y:=v^{\circ}_{1}\},\ldots,u^{\circ}_{n}\{y:=v^{\circ}_{n}\}\rangle}

Note that
\mathsf{labs}((R/t^{\prime})/(S/t^{\prime}))=\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}\}
.
Similarly,
\mathsf{labs}((R/S)/(t^{\prime}/S))=\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}\}
,
as required.

3.2.2

If
S
is in
p
.

Straightforward by
i.h.
.

3.2.3

If
S
is in
q
.

Straightforward since the steps are disjoint.

3.3

If
R
is in
q
.

We consider three subcases, depending on the position of
S
.

3.3.1

If
S
is at the root.
Then
p=\lambda x.s

and
t=(\lambda x.s)\mathtt{C}\langle(\lambda y.u)v\rangle
.

\textstyle{(\lambda x.s)\mathtt{C}\langle(\lambda y.u)v\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{S}
\textstyle{(\lambda x.s)\mathtt{C}\langle u\{y:=v\}\rangle}
\textstyle{s\{x:=\mathtt{C}\langle(\lambda y.u)v\rangle\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R/S}
\textstyle{s\{x:=\mathtt{C}\langle u\{y:=v\}\rangle\}}

\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces(\lambda^{\ell}x.s^{\prime})[\mathtt{C}_{i}\langle(\lambda^{\ell_{i,j}}y.u_{i,j}v_{i,j})\rangle_{j=1}^{m_{i}}]_{i=1}^{n}}
\scriptstyle{R/t^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{S/t^{\prime}}
\textstyle{(\lambda^{\ell}x.s^{\prime})[\mathtt{C}_{i}\langle u_{i,j}\{y:=v_{i,j}\}\rangle_{j=1}^{m_{i}}]_{i=1}^{n}}
\textstyle{s^{\prime}\{x:=[\mathtt{C}_{i}\langle(\lambda^{\ell_{i,j}}y.u_{i,j}v_{i,j})\rangle_{j=1}^{m_{i}}]_{i=1}^{n}\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(R/t^{\prime})/(S/t^{\prime})}
\textstyle{s^{\prime}\{x:=[\mathtt{C}_{i}\langle u_{i,j}\{y:=v_{i,j}\}\rangle_{j=1}^{m_{i}}]_{i=1}^{n}\}}

Note that
R/S
has as many elements as there are free occurrences
of
x
in
s
. In particular,
S
may erase or multiply
R
.
In turn
s^{\prime}
has a number of free occurrences of
x
,
more precisely
n
free occurrences of
x
,

i.e.
the cardinality of the argument of the application.
By Lem.
28
, each step in
R/S
,
when projected onto
t^{\prime}/S
yields a set of labels
\{\ell_{i,1},\ldots,\ell_{i,m_{i}}\}
.
So

\mathsf{labs}((R/S)/(t^{\prime}/S))=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{\ell_{i,1},\ldots,\ell_{i,m_{i}}\}=\mathsf{labs}((R/t^{\prime})/(S/t^{\prime}))

as required.

3.3.2

If
S
is in
p
.

Straightforward since the steps are disjoint.

3.3.3

If
S
is in
q
.

Straightforward by
i.h.
.

Lemma 30
(Simulation residual of a development)

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a set of coinitial steps in the
\lambda
-calculus,
let
\rho
be a complete development of
\mathcal{M}
,
and let
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
be a correct term such that
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathsf{src}(\rho)
.
Then
\rho/t^{\prime}
is a complete development of
\mathcal{M}/t^{\prime}
.

Proof

Recall that developments are finite and the
\lambda
-calculus is finitely branching so,
by König’s lemma, the length of a development of a set
\mathcal{M}
is bounded.
This bound is called the
depth
of
\mathcal{M}
.
We proceed by induction on the depth of
\mathcal{M}
.

1.

Base case.

Immediate.

2.

Induction.

Then
\mathcal{M}
must be non-empty. Let
\mathcal{M}=\{R_{1},\ldots,R_{n},S\}
for some
n\geq 0
,
and let
\rho
be a complete development of
\mathcal{M}
.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\rho=S\rho^{\prime}
,
where
\rho^{\prime}
is a complete development of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}/S
.
Note that the depth of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is strictly lesser than the depth of
\mathcal{M}
.
By
i.h.
,
\rho^{\prime}/(t^{\prime}/S)
is a complete development of

\mathcal{M}^{\prime}/(t^{\prime}/S)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\frac{R_{i}/S}{t^{\prime}/S}
.
By the Basic Cube Lemma (Lem.
29
) this set is equal to

\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\frac{R_{i}/t^{\prime}}{S/t^{\prime}}
.
Then
(S/t^{\prime})(\rho^{\prime}/(t^{\prime}/S))
is a complete development of

\mathcal{M}/t^{\prime}=(R_{1}/t^{\prime})\cup\ldots\cup(R_{n}/t^{\prime})\cup(S/t^{\prime})
.
To conclude, note that

\rho/t^{\prime}=S\rho^{\prime}/t^{\prime}=(S/t^{\prime})(\rho^{\prime}/(t^{\prime}/S))
so we are done.

Lemma 31
(Compatibility for developments)

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a set of coinitial steps,
and let
\rho
and
\sigma
be complete developments of
\mathcal{M}
,
and let
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
be a correct term such that
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathsf{src}(\rho)
.
Then
\rho/t^{\prime}\equiv\sigma/t^{\prime}
.

Proof

This is an immediate consequence of Lem.
30
,
since
\rho/t^{\prime}
and
\sigma/t^{\prime}
are both complete developments of
\mathcal{M}/t^{\prime}
,
hence permutation equivalent.

The proof of Prop.
8

proceeds as follows. Let
\rho\equiv\sigma
be permutation equivalent derivations in the
\lambda
-calculus,
let
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
be a correct term such that
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathsf{src}(\rho)
,
and let us show that:

1.

t^{\prime}/\rho=t^{\prime}/\sigma

2.

\rho/t^{\prime}\equiv\sigma/t^{\prime}

Recall that, in an orthogonal axiomatic rewrite system,
permutation equivalence may be defined as
the reflexive and transitive closure of the
permutation axiom

\tau_{1}R\sigma\tau_{2}\equiv\tau_{1}S\rho\tau_{2}
,
where

\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
are arbitrary derivations,

\sigma
is a complete development of
S/R
,
and
\rho
is a complete development of
R/S
.
For this definition of permutation equivalence, see for example
[
28
, Def. 2.17]
,
and .
We prove each item separately:

1.

For the first item, we proceed by induction on the derivation that
\rho\equiv\sigma
.
Reflexivity and transitivity are trivial, so we concentrate on the permutation axiom itself.
Let
\tau_{1}R\sigma\tau_{2}\equiv\tau_{1}S\rho\tau_{2}
,
where

\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
are arbitrary derivations,

\sigma
is a complete development of
S/R
,
and
\rho
is a complete development of
R/S
,
and let us show that
t^{\prime}/\tau_{1}R\sigma\tau_{2}=t^{\prime}/\tau_{1}S\rho\tau_{2}
.
By Lem.
27
we have that:

t^{\prime}/\tau_{1}R\sigma\tau_{2}=((t^{\prime}/\tau_{1})/R\sigma)/\tau_{2}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{ and }\hskip 14.22636ptt^{\prime}/\tau_{1}S\rho\tau_{2}=((t^{\prime}/\tau_{1})/S\rho)/\tau_{2}

so, without loss of generality,
it suffices to show that for an arbitrary term
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
,
we have
s^{\prime}/R\sigma=s^{\prime}/S\rho
.
By definition of simulation residual, the derivations below have the indicated sources and targets:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}R\sigma/s^{\prime}&:&s^{\prime}\to s^{\prime}/R\sigma\\
S\rho/s^{\prime}&:&s^{\prime}\to s^{\prime}/S\rho\\
\end{array}

Moreover:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}R\sigma/s^{\prime}&=&(R/s^{\prime})(\sigma/(s^{\prime}/R))\\
&\equiv&(R/s^{\prime})((S/R)/(s^{\prime}/R))&\text{by Lem.~\ref{lemma:compatibility_for_developments}}\\
&\equiv&(R/s^{\prime})((S/s^{\prime})/(R/s^{\prime}))&\text{by the basic cube lemma~(Lem.~\ref{lemma:cube_lemma_for_simulation_residuals})}\\
&\equiv&(S/s^{\prime})((R/s^{\prime})/(S/s^{\prime}))&\text{since $A(B/A)\equiv B(A/B)$ holds in general}\\
&\equiv&(S/s^{\prime})((R/S)/(s^{\prime}/S))&\text{by the basic cube lemma~(Lem.~\ref{lemma:cube_lemma_for_simulation_residuals})}\\
&=&(S/s^{\prime})(\rho/(s^{\prime}/S))&\text{by Lem.~\ref{lemma:compatibility_for_developments}}\\
&=&S\rho/s^{\prime}\end{array}

So
R\sigma/s^{\prime}
and
S\rho/s^{\prime}
are permutation equivalent.
In particular, they have the same target, so

s^{\prime}/R\sigma=s^{\prime}/S\rho
as required.

2.

The proof of the second item is also by induction on the derivation that
\rho\equiv\sigma
.
Let
\tau_{1}R\sigma\tau_{2}\equiv\tau_{1}S\rho\tau_{2}
,
where

\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
are arbitrary derivations,

\sigma
is a complete development of
S/R
,
and
\rho
is a complete development of
R/S
,
and let us show that
\tau_{1}R\sigma\tau_{2}/t^{\prime}=\tau_{1}S\rho\tau_{2}/t^{\prime}
.
By Lem.
27
we have that:

\tau_{1}R\sigma\tau_{2}/t^{\prime}=(\tau_{1}/t^{\prime})(R\sigma/s^{\prime})(\tau_{2}/u^{\prime})\hskip 14.22636pt\text{ and }\hskip 14.22636pt\tau_{1}S\rho\tau_{2}/t^{\prime}=(\tau_{1}/t^{\prime})(S\rho/s^{\prime})(\tau_{2}/u^{\prime\prime})

where
s^{\prime}=t^{\prime}/\tau_{1}
,

u^{\prime}=s^{\prime}/\tau_{1}R\sigma
,
and

u^{\prime\prime}=s^{\prime}/\tau_{1}S\rho
.
Similarly as before, we can prove that
R\sigma/s^{\prime}\equiv S\rho/s^{\prime}
.
By item 1 of this proposition, we have
u^{\prime}=u^{\prime\prime}
,
so
\tau_{2}/u^{\prime}\equiv\tau_{2}/u^{\prime\prime}
,
which finishes the proof.

0.A.10
Proof of Lem.
4
– Cube Lemma for simulation residuals

Let
\rho:t\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}}s
and
\sigma:t\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}}u
be coinitial derivations,
and let
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\texttt{{\#}}}
be a correct term such that
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
.
The statement of Lem.
4
claims that the following equivalence holds:

(\rho/t^{\prime})/(\sigma/t^{\prime})\equiv(\rho/\sigma)/(t^{\prime}/\sigma)

Before proving Lem.
4
,
we prove three auxiliary lemmas, all of which are particular cases of the main result.

Lemma 32

(R/t^{\prime})/(\sigma/t^{\prime})\equiv(R/\sigma)/(t^{\prime}/\sigma)
.

Proof

By induction on
\sigma
:

1.

Empty,
\sigma=\epsilon
.

Immediate since
R/t^{\prime}=R/t^{\prime}
.

2.

Non-empty,
\sigma=S\sigma^{\prime}
.

Then:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}(R/t^{\prime})/(S\sigma^{\prime}/t^{\prime})&=&(R/t^{\prime})/((S/t^{\prime})(\sigma^{\prime}/(t^{\prime}/S)))&\text{ by definition}\\
&=&((R/t^{\prime})/(S/t^{\prime}))/(\sigma^{\prime}/(t^{\prime}/S))&\text{ by definition}\\
&\equiv&((R/S)/(t^{\prime}/S))/(\sigma^{\prime}/(t^{\prime}/S))&\text{ by Lem.~\ref{lemma:cube_lemma_for_simulation_residuals}}\\
&\equiv&((R/S)/\sigma^{\prime})/((t^{\prime}/S)/\sigma^{\prime})&\text{ by {i.h.} }\\
&=&(R/S\sigma^{\prime})/(t^{\prime}/S\sigma^{\prime})&\text{ by definition}\end{array}

Lemma 33

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a set of coinitial steps in the
\lambda
-calculus.
Then
(\mathcal{M}/t^{\prime})/(S/t^{\prime})
and
(\mathcal{M}/S)/(t^{\prime}/S)

are equal as sets.

Proof

Using Lem.
29
:

(\mathcal{M}/t^{\prime})/(S/t^{\prime})=\bigcup_{R\in\mathcal{M}}(R/t^{\prime})/(S/t^{\prime})=\bigcup_{R\in\mathcal{M}}(R/S)/(t^{\prime}/S)=(\mathcal{M}/S)/(t^{\prime}/S)

Lemma 34

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a set of coinitial steps in the
\lambda
-calculus,
and let
\mathcal{M}
also stand for some (canonical) complete development of
\mathcal{M}
.
Then
(\rho/t^{\prime})/(\mathcal{M}/t^{\prime})\equiv(\rho/\mathcal{M})/(t^{\prime}/\mathcal{M})
.

Proof

By induction on
\rho
:

1.

Empty,
\rho=\epsilon
.

Immediate since
\epsilon=\epsilon
.

2.

Non-empty,
\rho=R\rho^{\prime}
.

Then:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}(R\rho^{\prime}/t^{\prime})/(\mathcal{M}/t^{\prime})&=&(R/t^{\prime})(\rho^{\prime}/(t^{\prime}/R))/(\mathcal{M}/t^{\prime})&\text{ by definition}\\
&=&((R/t^{\prime})/(\mathcal{M}/t^{\prime}))((\rho^{\prime}/(t^{\prime}/R))/((\mathcal{M}/t^{\prime})/(R/t^{\prime})))&\text{ by definition}\\
&\equiv&((R/\mathcal{M})/(t^{\prime}/\mathcal{M}))((\rho^{\prime}/(t^{\prime}/R))/((\mathcal{M}/t^{\prime})/(R/t^{\prime})))&\text{ by Lem.~\ref{lemma:generalized_cube_lemma__claim1}}\\
&=&((R/\mathcal{M})/(t^{\prime}/\mathcal{M}))((\rho^{\prime}/(t^{\prime}/R))/((\mathcal{M}/R)/(t^{\prime}/R)))&\text{ by Lem.~\ref{lemma:generalized_cube_lemma__claim2}}\\
&=&((R/\mathcal{M})/(t^{\prime}/\mathcal{M}))((\rho^{\prime}/(\mathcal{M}/R))/((t^{\prime}/R)/(\mathcal{M}/R)))&\text{ by {i.h.} }\\
&=&((R/\mathcal{M})/(t^{\prime}/\mathcal{M}))((\rho^{\prime}/(\mathcal{M}/R))/((t^{\prime}/\mathcal{M})/(R/\mathcal{M})))&\text{ by Prop.~\ref{prop:compatibility_of_simulation_residuals_and_permutation_equivalence} ($\star$)}\\
&=&(R/\mathcal{M})(\rho^{\prime}/(\mathcal{M}/R))/(t^{\prime}/\mathcal{M})&\text{ by definition}\\
&=&(R\rho^{\prime}/\mathcal{M})/(t^{\prime}/\mathcal{M})&\text{ by definition}\\
\end{array}

For the equality marked with (
\star
), observe that

(t^{\prime}/R)/(\mathcal{M}/R)=t^{\prime}/(R\sqcup\mathcal{M})

and

(t^{\prime}/\mathcal{M})/(R/\mathcal{M})=t^{\prime}/(\mathcal{M}\sqcup R)

by definition.
Moreover
R\sqcup\mathcal{M}\equiv\mathcal{M}\sqcup R
,
so by Compatibility (Prop.
8
),

(t^{\prime}/R)/(\mathcal{M}/R)=(t^{\prime}/\mathcal{M})/(R/\mathcal{M})
.

Now the proof of Lem.
4
proceeds by induction on
\rho
:

1.

Empty
\rho=\epsilon
.
Immediate since
\epsilon=\epsilon
.

2.

Non-empty,
\rho=R\rho^{\prime}
.
Then:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}(R\rho^{\prime}/\sigma)/(t^{\prime}/\sigma)&=&(R/\sigma)(\rho^{\prime}/(\sigma/R))/(t^{\prime}/\sigma)&\text{ by definition}\\
&=&((R/\sigma)/(t^{\prime}/\sigma))((\rho^{\prime}/(\sigma/R))/((t^{\prime}/\sigma)/(R/\sigma)))&\text{ by definition}\\
&\equiv&((R/t^{\prime})/(\sigma/t^{\prime}))((\rho^{\prime}/(\sigma/R))/((t^{\prime}/\sigma)/(R/\sigma)))&\text{ by Lem.~\ref{lemma:generalized_cube_lemma__claim1}}\\
&=&((R/t^{\prime})/(\sigma/t^{\prime}))((\rho^{\prime}/(\sigma/R))/((t^{\prime}/R)/(\sigma/R)))&\text{ by Prop.~\ref{prop:compatibility_of_simulation_residuals_and_permutation_equivalence} ($\star$)}\\
&=&((R/t^{\prime})/(\sigma/t^{\prime}))((\rho^{\prime}/(t^{\prime}/R))/((\sigma/R)/(t^{\prime}/R)))&\text{ by {i.h.} }\\
&=&((R/t^{\prime})/(\sigma/t^{\prime}))((\rho^{\prime}/(t^{\prime}/R))/((\sigma/t^{\prime})/(R/t^{\prime})))&\text{ by Lem.~\ref{lemma:generalized_cube_lemma__claim3}}\\
&=&(R/t^{\prime})(\rho^{\prime}/(t^{\prime}/R))/(\sigma/t^{\prime})&\text{ by definition}\\
&=&(R\rho^{\prime}/t^{\prime})/(\sigma/t^{\prime})&\text{ by definition}\\
\end{array}

For the equality marked with (
\star
), observe that

(t^{\prime}/\sigma)/(R/\sigma)=t^{\prime}/(\sigma\sqcup R)

and

(t^{\prime}/R)/(\sigma/R)=t^{\prime}/(R\sqcup\sigma)

by definition.
Moreover
\sigma\sqcup R\equiv R\sqcup\sigma
,
so by Compatibility (Prop.
8
),

(t^{\prime}/\sigma)/(R/\sigma)=(t^{\prime}/R)/(\sigma/R)
.

0.A.11
Proof of Prop.
9
– Properties of garbage

1.

Let
\sigma\sqsubseteq\rho
.
Then
\sigma\tau\equiv\rho
for some
\tau
,
so
\sigma/t^{\prime}\sqsubseteq(\sigma/t^{\prime})(\tau/(t^{\prime}/\sigma))=\sigma\tau/t^{\prime}\equiv\rho/t^{\prime}

by
Compatibility
(Prop.
8
).

2.

Note that
\rho\sigma/t^{\prime}=(\rho/t^{\prime})(\sigma/(t^{\prime}/\rho))
.
So
\rho\sigma/t^{\prime}
is empty if and only if

\rho/t^{\prime}
and
\sigma/(t^{\prime}/\rho)
are empty.

3.

Suppose that
\rho/t^{\prime}=\epsilon
.
Then
(\rho/\sigma)/(t^{\prime}/\sigma)=(\rho/t^{\prime})/(\sigma/t^{\prime})

by the
Cube Lemma
(Lem.
4
).

4.

By the
Cube Lemma
(Lem.
4
):

(\rho\sqcup\sigma)/t^{\prime}=\rho(\sigma/\rho)/t^{\prime}=(\rho/t^{\prime})((\sigma/\rho)/(t^{\prime}/\rho)\equiv(\rho/t^{\prime})((\sigma/t^{\prime})/(\rho/t^{\prime}))=(\rho/t^{\prime})\sqcup(\sigma/t^{\prime})
.
So
(\rho\sqcup\sigma)/t^{\prime}
is empty if and only if

\rho/t^{\prime}
and
\sigma/t^{\prime}
are empty.

0.A.12
Proof of Lem.
5
– Sieving is well-defined

Note that
t^{\prime}/R_{0}\mathrel{\ltimes}\rho/R_{0}
by definition of simulation residual,
so the recursive call can be made.

To see that recursion is well-founded, consider the measure
given by
M(\rho,t^{\prime})=\#\mathsf{labs}(\rho/t^{\prime})
.
Let
R_{0}
be a coarse step for
(\rho,t^{\prime})
.
Observe that
R_{0}\sqsubseteq\rho

so
R_{0}/t^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\rho/t^{\prime}
by Coro.
2
.
Then
\mathsf{labs}(R_{0}/t^{\prime})\subseteq\mathsf{labs}(\rho/t^{\prime})
by Prop.
12
.
So we have:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\#\mathsf{labs}(\rho/t^{\prime})&>&\#(\mathsf{labs}(\rho/t^{\prime})\setminus\mathsf{labs}(R_{0}/t^{\prime}))&\text{since $R_{0}/t^{\prime}\neq\varnothing$ and $\mathsf{labs}(R_{0}/t^{\prime})\subseteq\mathsf{labs}(\rho/t^{\prime})$}\\
&=&\#\mathsf{labs}((\rho/t^{\prime})/(R_{0}/t^{\prime}))&\text{by Lem.~\ref{lemma:names_after_projection_along_a_step}}\\
&=&\#\mathsf{labs}((\rho/R_{0})/(t^{\prime}/R_{0}))\end{array}

as required.

0.A.13
Proof of Prop.
10
– Properties of sieving

To prove Prop.
10
we first prove various auxiliary results.

Lemma 35
(The sieve is a prefix)

Let
\rho:t\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}^{*}s
and
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
.
Then
\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\rho
.

Proof

By induction on the length of
\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime}
.
If there are no coarse steps for
(\rho,t^{\prime})
,
then trivially
\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime}=\epsilon\sqsubseteq\rho
.
If there is a coarse step for
(\rho,t^{\prime})
,
let
R_{0}
be the leftmost such step. Then:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime}&=&R_{0}((\rho/R_{0})\Downarrow(t^{\prime}/R_{0}))\\
&\sqsubseteq&R_{0}(\rho/R_{0})&\text{by {i.h.} }\\
&\equiv&\rho(R_{0}/\rho)&\text{since $A(B/A)\equiv B(A/B)$ in general}\\
&=&\rho&\text{since $R_{0}\sqsubseteq\rho$ as $R_{0}$ is coarse for $(\rho,t^{\prime})$}\\
\end{array}

We also need the following technical lemma:

Lemma 36
(Refinement of a context)

The following are equivalent:

1.

t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}\langle s\rangle
,

2.

t^{\prime}
is of the form
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}\rangle
,
where
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}
is an
n
-hole context such that
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}
and

s^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\ltimes}s
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.
Note that
n
might be
0
, in which case
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}
is a term.

Moreover, in the implication
(1\implies 2)
,
the decomposition is unique,
i.e. the context
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}
,
the number of holes
n\geq 0
,
and the terms
s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{n}

are the unique possible such objects.

Proof

Straightforward by induction on
\mathtt{C}
.

Lemma 37
(Garbage only interacts with garbage)

The following hold:

1.

Garbage only creates garbage.

Let
R
and
S
be composable steps in the
\lambda
-calculus,
and let
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathsf{src}(R)
.
If
R
creates
S
and
R
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage,
then
S
is
(t^{\prime}/R)
-garbage.

2.

Garbage only duplicates garbage.

Let
R
and
S
be coinitial steps in the
\lambda
-calculus
and let
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathsf{src}(R)
.
If
R
duplicates
S
, i.e.
\#(S/R)>1
,
and
R
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage,
then
S
is
(t^{\prime}/R)
-garbage.

Proof

We prove each item separately:

1.

According to Lévy
[
25
]
,
there are three creation cases in the
\lambda
-calculus.
We consider the three possibilities for
R
creating
S
:

1.1

Case I,

\mathtt{C}\langle(\lambda x.x)(\lambda y.s)u\rangle\mathrel{\xrightarrow{R}_{\beta}}\mathtt{C}\langle(\lambda y.s)u\rangle\mathrel{\xrightarrow{S}_{\beta}}\mathtt{C}\langle s\{y:=u\}\rangle
.

Then by Lem.
36
,
the term
t^{\prime}
is of the form

\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{n}\rangle

where
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}
is an
n
-hole context such that
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}\langle\Box\,u\rangle

and
\Delta_{i}\mathrel{\ltimes}(\lambda x.x)(\lambda y.s)
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.
Since
R
is garbage, we know that actually
n=0
.
So
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}\langle\Box\,u\rangle
and
R/t^{\prime}:t^{\prime}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow_{\texttt{{\#}}}}t^{\prime}=t^{\prime}/R
in zero steps.
Hence
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}\langle(\lambda y.s)u\rangle
,
so by Lem.
36
,
the term
t^{\prime}
can be written in a unique way as

\mathtt{C}^{\prime\prime}\langle\Sigma_{1},\ldots,\Sigma_{m}\rangle
, where
\mathtt{C}^{\prime\prime}
is an
m
-hole context
such that
\mathtt{C}^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}\langle\Box\,u\rangle
and
\Sigma_{i}\mathrel{\ltimes}\lambda y.s
for all
1\leq i\leq m
.
Since the decomposition is unique and
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}\langle\Box\,u\rangle
,
we conclude that
m=0
.
Hence
S
is
(t^{\prime}/R)
-garbage.

1.2

Case II,

\mathtt{C}\langle(\lambda x.\lambda y.s)\,u\,r\rangle\mathrel{\xrightarrow{R}_{\beta}}\mathtt{C}\langle(\lambda y.s\{x:=u\})\,r\rangle\mathrel{\xrightarrow{S}_{\beta}}\mathtt{C}\langle s\{x:=u\}\{y:=r\}\rangle
.

Then by Lem.
36
,
the term
t^{\prime}
is of the form

\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{n}\rangle

where
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}
is an
n
-hole context such that
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}\langle\Box\,r\rangle

and
\Delta_{i}\mathrel{\ltimes}(\lambda x.\lambda y.s)\,u
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.
Since
R
is garbage, we know that actually
n=0
.
So
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}\langle\Box\,r\rangle
and
R/t^{\prime}:t^{\prime}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow_{\texttt{{\#}}}}t^{\prime}=t^{\prime}/R

in zero steps. Hence
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}\langle(\lambda y.s\{x:=u\})\,r\rangle
,
so by Lem.
36
, the term
t^{\prime}
can be written in a unique way as

\mathtt{C}^{\prime\prime}\langle\Sigma_{1},\ldots,\Sigma_{n}\rangle
, where
\mathtt{C}^{\prime\prime}
is an
m
-hole context such that

\mathtt{C}^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}\langle\Box\,r\rangle
and
\Sigma_{i}\mathrel{\ltimes}\lambda y.s\{x:=u\}

for all
1\leq i\leq m
.
Since the decomposition is unique and
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}\langle\Box\,r\rangle
,
we conclude that
m=0
.
Hence
S
is
(t^{\prime}/R)
-garbage.

1.3

Case III,
\mathtt{C}_{1}\langle(\lambda x.\mathtt{C}_{2}\langle x\,s\rangle)\,(\lambda y.u)\rangle\mathrel{\xrightarrow{R}_{\beta}}\mathtt{C}_{1}\langle\hat{\mathtt{C}}_{2}\langle(\lambda y.u)\,\hat{s}\rangle\rangle\mathrel{\xrightarrow{S}_{\beta}}\mathtt{C}_{1}\langle\hat{\mathtt{C}}_{2}\langle u\{y:=\hat{s}\}\rangle\rangle
,
where

\hat{\mathtt{C}}_{2}=\mathtt{C}_{2}\{x:=\lambda y.u\}

and

\hat{t}=t\{x:=\lambda y.u\}
.

Then by Lem.
36
,
the term
t^{\prime}
is of the form
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{n}\rangle

where
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}
is an
n
-hole context such that
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}_{1}

and
\Delta_{i}\mathrel{\ltimes}(\lambda x.\mathtt{C}_{2}\langle x\,s\rangle)\,(\lambda y.u)
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.
Since
R
is garbage, we know that actually
n=0
.
So
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}_{1}
and
R/t^{\prime}:t^{\prime}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow_{\texttt{{\#}}}}t^{\prime}=t^{\prime}/R
in zero steps.
Hence
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}_{1}\langle\hat{\mathtt{C}}_{2}\langle(\lambda y.u)\,\hat{s}\rangle\rangle
,
so by Lem.
36
, the term
t^{\prime}
can be written in a unique way as

\mathtt{C}^{\prime\prime}\langle\Sigma_{1},\ldots,\Sigma_{m}\rangle
, where
\mathtt{C}^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}_{1}
and

\Sigma_{i}\mathrel{\ltimes}\hat{\mathtt{C}}_{2}\langle(\lambda y.u)\,\hat{s}\rangle
for all
1\leq i\leq m
.
Since the decomposition is unique and
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}_{1}
, we conclude that
m=0
.
Hence
S
is
(t^{\prime}/R)
-garbage.

2.

Since
R
duplicates
S
,
the redex contracted by
S

lies inside the argument of
R
, that is,
the source term is of the form
\mathtt{C}_{1}\langle(\lambda x.t)\mathtt{C}_{2}\langle(\lambda y.s)u\rangle\rangle

where the pattern of
R
is
(\lambda x.t)\mathtt{C}_{2}\langle(\lambda y.s)u\rangle
,
and the pattern of
S
is
(\lambda y.s)u
.
By Lem.
36
,
the term
t^{\prime}
is of the form
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\langle\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{n}\rangle

where
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}
is an
n
-hole context
such that
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}
and
\Delta_{i}\mathrel{\ltimes}(\lambda x.t)\mathtt{C}_{2}\langle(\lambda y.s)u\rangle
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.
Since
R
is garbage, we know that
n=0
.
By Lem.
36
,
the term
t^{\prime}
can be written as
\mathtt{C}^{\prime\prime}\langle\Sigma_{1},\ldots,\Sigma_{m}\rangle

where
\mathtt{C}^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}_{1}\langle(\lambda x.t)\mathtt{C}_{2}\rangle

and
\Sigma_{i}\mathrel{\ltimes}(\lambda y.s)u
for all
1\leq i\leq m
.
Note that
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}_{1}
so
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}_{1}\langle(\lambda x.t)\mathtt{C}_{2}\rangle
,
as can be checked by induction on
\mathtt{C}_{1}
.
Since the decomposition is unique, this means that
m=0
,
and thus
S
is garbage.

Proposition 13
(Characterization of garbage)

Let
\rho:t\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}}s
and
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}t
.
The following are equivalent:

1.

\rho\Downarrow t^{\prime}=\epsilon
.

2.

There are no coarse steps for
(\rho,t^{\prime})
.

3.

The derivation
\rho
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage.

Proof

It is immediate to check that items 1 and 2 are equivalent, by definition of sieving,
so let us prove
2\implies 3
and
3\implies 2
:

•

(2\implies 3)

We prove the contrapositive, namely that if
\rho
is not garbage,
there is a coarse step for
(\rho,t^{\prime})
.
Suppose that
\rho
is not garbage,
i.e.

\rho/t^{\prime}\neq\epsilon
.
Then by Prop.
9

we have that
\rho
can be written as
\rho=\rho_{1}R\rho_{2}
where
all the steps in
\rho_{1}
are garbage and
R
is not garbage.
By the fact that garbage only creates garbage (Lem.
37
)
the step
R
has an ancestor
R_{0}
,
i.e.

R\in R_{0}/\rho_{1}
.
Moreover, since garbage only duplicates garbage (Lem.
37
)
we have that
R_{0}/\rho_{1}=R
.
Given that
R
is not garbage, we have that:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}(R_{0}/t^{\prime})/(\rho_{1}/t^{\prime})&=&(R_{0}/\rho_{1})/(t^{\prime}/\rho_{1})&\text{ by Lem.~\ref{lemma:generalized_cube_lemma}}\\
&=&R/(t^{\prime}/\rho_{1})\\
&\neq&\varnothing\\
\end{array}

Since
(R_{0}/t^{\prime})/(\rho_{1}/t^{\prime})\neq\varnothing
, in particular,

R_{0}/t^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
, which means that
R_{0}
is not garbage.
Moreover,
R_{0}\sqsubseteq\rho_{1}R\rho_{2}=\rho
.
So
R_{0}
is coarse for
(\rho,t^{\prime})
.

•

(3\implies 2)

Let
\rho
be garbage, suppose that there is a coarse step
R
for
(\rho,t^{\prime})
,
and let us derive a contradiction.
Since
R
is coarse for
(\rho,t^{\prime})
,
we have that
R\sqsubseteq\rho
,
so
R/t^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\rho/t^{\prime}
by Coro.
2
.
But
\rho/t^{\prime}
is empty because
\rho
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage,
that is,
R/t^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\sigma/t^{\prime}=\epsilon
,
which means that
R
is also
t^{\prime}
-garbage.
This contradicts the fact that
R
is coarse for
(\rho,t^{\prime})
.

Lemma 38
(The leftmost coarse step has at most one residual)

Let
R_{0}
be the leftmost coarse step for
(\rho,t^{\prime})
,
and let
\sigma\sqsubseteq\rho
.
Then
\#(R_{0}/\sigma)\leq 1
.

Proof

By induction on the length of
\sigma
. The base case is immediate.
For the inductive step, let
\sigma=S\tau\sqsubseteq\rho
.
Then in particular
S\sqsubseteq\rho
.
We consider two cases, depending on whether
R_{0}=S
.

1.

Equal,
R_{0}=S
.

Then
R_{0}/\sigma=R_{0}/R_{0}\tau=\varnothing
and we are done.

2.

Non-equal,
R_{0}\neq S
.

First we argue that
R_{0}/S
has exactly one residual
R_{1}\in R_{0}/S
.
To see this, we consider two further cases, depending on whether

S
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage or not:

2.1

If
S
is not
t^{\prime}
-garbage.

Then
S\sqsubseteq\rho
and
S/t^{\prime}=\varnothing
, so
S
is coarse for
(\rho,t^{\prime})
.
Since
R_{0}
is the leftmost coarse step, this means that
R
is to the left of
S
.
So
R_{0}
has exactly one residual
R_{1}\in R_{0}/S
.

2.2

If
S
is
t^{\prime}
-garbage.

Let us write the term
t
as
t=\mathtt{C}\langle(\lambda x.s)u\rangle
, where

(\lambda x.s)u
is the pattern of the redex
S
.
By Lem.
36

the term
t^{\prime}
is of the form
t^{\prime}=\mathtt{C}^{\prime}[\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{n}]
,
where
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}
is a many-hole context such that
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}\mathrel{\ltimes}\mathtt{C}

and
\Delta_{i}\mathrel{\ltimes}(\lambda x.s)u
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.
We know that
S
is garbage, so
n=0
and
t^{\prime}=\mathtt{C}^{\prime}
is actually
a
0
-hole context (
i.e.
